13C. Hagar branch & the Robert Larry filings — document-level findings (October 21, 2026)

New research · two branches read in full from the recorded instruments

The two branches below were read directly from the recorded images rather than from prior summaries. Each reading is tagged as a record statement or as a conflict. Nothing here establishes wrongdoing by any living person. What it does establish is a series of specific, checkable statements on the county record that conflict with other records, plus the gaps that a title examiner or investigator would need closed.

1. The Hagar branch — what the 1986 Tresimond Corbello affidavit actually says

The 1986 affidavit (OPR Vol. 622 pp. 767–768, instrument 39480, filed June 16, 1986, sworn June 12 before a Calcasieu Parish notary) has now been read in full. Its contents are narrower and more specific than earlier summaries indicated:

  • Affiants: William Roel Hagar and Jo Ann Benniefiel, both of Calcasieu Parish, Louisiana, describing themselves as “the surviving grandchildren of Tresimond Corbello, deceased.”
  • Decedent: Tresimond Corbello died intestate at Lake Charles on November 29, 1956; married once, to Sarah Cole Corbello.
  • Two children only: Lillie May Corbello Hagar Bertrand (b. 7/11/06) and Josephine Blanche Corbello Benniefiel (b. 7/23/08). Lillie May died August 23, 1984; her children are William Roel Hagar, John Roy Hagar, Arthur Roland Hagar, plus adopted Billy Gene Bertrand. Josephine Blanche died January 22, 1986; her child is Jack S. Benniefiel, plus adopted Jo Ann Benniefiel. The affidavit states there were no other children born to or adopted by any of the three.
  • Claim: Tresimond held, at death, “an undivided 1/8 interest in the surface and mineral estate” of the 300-acre Block No. 3, and that the named descendants “constitute the sole living heirs at law, all of whom are above the age of eighteen.”
  • Administration: “There was no administration on the estate of Tresimond Corbello, nor was any necessary.”

Why this narrows the Hagar branch: the affiants are grandchildren, not the two daughters. The surviving adult line is therefore the Hagar and Benniefiel grandchildren, and the branch’s own document self-describes as a 1/8 share of the 300 acres — the same 1/8 share claimed in the 1986 affidavits for other children of Erisse. Combined with the 1931 mineral deeds (below), the Hagar branch appears to hold a surface interest whose mineral interest was separately conveyed in 1931. Verify whether Lillie May’s and Josephine Blanche’s estates were ever opened in Calcasieu Parish, and whether any 1956–1986 conveyance of their surface interest exists.

Independent corroboration of the Hagar name: a public genealogy index records William Roel Hagar (b. December 23, 1921, Ragley, Calcasieu Parish) as the son of John T Hagar and Lillie M Corbello, with a Texas-issued Social Security number and death in July 1994 in the Lake Charles area. This matches the 1986 affidavit’s affiant and confirms the Hagar spelling for the family line, distinct from the Houston oilman Lee Hager of Federal Royalty Company discussed in section 7.

Read from Tresimond Corbello/affadavit_of_heirship.pdf (4 pages) and the staged records/1986-06-16_OPR-622-p767_39480_Heirship-Tresimond-Corbello.pdf. Corroborating name detail: public genealogy index entry for William Roel Hagar. No Orange County or Calcasieu Parish record of a Hagar/Benniefiel conveyance has been located; only the affidavit.

2. The 1931 mineral deeds — a grantor list that bears directly on the Hagar question

The three November 1931 mineral deeds to J. H. Harlan were read in full (DR Vol. 52 pp. 123–127, corrected at pp. 130–133 and pp. 171–). The grantor list is identical across all three:

Mary LeBleu and husband Colin LeBleu; Angeline Champyne and husband D. Champyne; Dorie Savoie and husband Tan Savoie; T. C. Carbello; A. Carbello; Martin Corbello; and Clairese Lovett and husband Sol Lovett — all residents of Calcasieu Parish, Louisiana, conveying for $10.00 their “all undivided interest in the oil, gas and other minerals” in the 50-acre John Granger tract (Subdivisions 2 and 3), warranted at “not less than an undivided 2.188 acres,” and reserving to the grantors a 1/96 net royalty on production. T. C. Carbello is Tresimond Corbello. The notarization of his signature was witnessed by Mrs. J. S. Benniefiel — the Hagar/Benniefiel family connection appears on the 1931 mineral deed itself.

Direct consequence for the disputed-claim question: the 1931 deeds show seven of Erisse’s eight children conveying their mineral interests in 1931, with Tresimond among them. Any claim that the Hagar branch still holds a full undivided 1/8 mineral interest in the 300 acres is in tension with the 1931 conveyances. The surface and mineral estates must be analyzed separately — the 1924 judgment also split surface title 1/2 to Erisse and 1/2 to Wasey while compromising the producing tracts. This is the specific point a title examiner would need resolved, and it applies to every branch, not only Hagar.

Read from the staged records/1931-11-05_DR-52-p123…, …-p130… and …-p171… PDFs (OCR of the full recorded images).

3. The Robert Larry filing series — four instruments read in full

The Robert Oval Corbello branch has a coherent, self-consistent paper trail. The 2001 instruments are the point of interest, because they make sworn statements that conflict with other records.

The described tract is not the ancestral 300 acres and is unrelated to Block 3 on its face. What the pattern is for — estate-closing formality, lien or title-vendor requirements, or something else — is not established by the documents. It is a documented structural oddity worth asking the family and the preparing attorney about.

Dec. 22, 1958, Nederland, Texas). Attached is a different legal description — a metes-and-bounds parcel opening at the southeast corner of the Mose Granger 50-acre tract, 711.18 feet, 495.47 feet, 555.55 feet, 1,139.94 feet, containing 8.13 acres — plus a hand-drawn plat, a reference to a 1959 Orange County District Court judgment “Martin Corbello et al. v. Novello et al., Gulf Pipe Line Co. et al.” at page 366 et seq. of the district court minutes, and the same 1/5-of-1/8 recital.The Martin Corbello line. Note the internal inconsistency: the Robert Oval affidavit lists two children of Martin (Robert Oval and Linnie M. If Robert Oval and Linnie are Erisse’s grandchildren, then Robert Larry and Jerry Dean inherit through their father only, and the filings’ “only surviving heirs” language covers Erisse and Martin but not the other six branches.

4. Documented conflicts arising from the 2001 filings

Statement in the 2001 filingsConflicting recordStrength of the conflict
Erisse died February 24, 1925, at Allen, Louisiana, buried “in Hayes La., Calcasieu Parish,” per “Mrs Joe Lucas.”The Lake Charles American Press obituary of February 7, 1930 (transcribed in the USGenWeb Calcasieu obituary archive): she died at her home at 2002 Moeling Street “this morning (Feb. 3, 1930) at 9:05 o’clock,” aged 84, survived by eight children (Tresimon, Mary Modest, Artemon, Angeline, Josephine, Clarisse, Martin, Mary Dorise), burial in Sallier cemetery. The Louisiana Supreme Court in Corbello v. Corbello, 171 La. 735 (1930), also fixes her death at February 3, 1930 and adjudicated the curatorship of her estate.Strong. A published 1930 newspaper obituary and a 1930 state appellate opinion both contradict the 1925 date. The 1925 date is attributed only to a family informant, “Mrs Joe Lucas.”
Erisse had only two children: Martin and Trezimond Corbello.The same 1930 obituary names eight surviving children. The 1931 mineral deeds were executed by seven of them (LeBleu, Champyne, Savoie, T. C. Carbello, A. Carbello, Martin Corbello, Lovett). The 1931 deeds are dated after the asserted February 1925 death, so a 1925 death is inconsistent with the 1931 instruments unless the filers are relying on a death date that would make those deeds void or voidable.Strong. Directly contradicted by a contemporaneous newspaper, a state appellate opinion, and seven contemporaneous deed instruments.
Robert Larry and Jerry Dean are “the only surviving heirs” of Erisse.Seven other branches filed heirship affidavits on the same county roll: Duhon (#38603), Savoie (#39110), Tresimond (#39480), LeBleu (#39522), Artemon (#39694), Robert Oval (#41237) and Clarise Lovett (#43273) in 1986, plus the 2006 Lovett batch (#298552–#298565). The 2001 filings were filed after those, and after the 1931 deeds that seven of those eight children signed.Strong. The claim is inconsistent with the county’s own index for documents filed 15 years earlier.
Erisse was survived by her husband Charles Corbello, and “Charles Corbello is 1 of 8 children born to Alphonse Corbello, deceased. Mother Marie Anette Corbello.”Charles/Manuel Carlos Corbello is elsewhere documented as a son of Encarnacion Yldefonso Curbelo and Marie La Mirande (compiled report), or as a son of Jose Curbelo and Adelaide De La Fosse — the report itself lists him twice under two different parents. “Alphonse” and “Marie Anette” appear in no other source. The 1930 obituary identifies him simply as “Chas. Corbello.”Open. This is the one 2001 statement that is not resolved by a record, and it points to a third parentage set.
No administration was had on Erisse’s estate.Erisse was interdicted in 1925 and curated by Calcasieu National Bank of Southwest Louisiana until her 1930 death; the curator’s final account was reviewed by the Louisiana Supreme Court in Corbello v. Corbello. Ancillary guardianship was opened in Orange County Probate Case No. 680.Strong, with a nuance. A curatorship is not the same as a probate administration, so the statement is imprecise rather than plainly false — but it omits a documented five-year court-supervised curatorship and the state appellate proceeding over that estate’s accounts.
Robert Larry’s and Jerry Dean’s interest is 1/5 of the 1/8, in Abst. 10 Tracts 2 and 3, conveyed by warranty deed.The 1924 judgment already fixed the surface estate as 1/2 to Erisse and 1/2 to Wasey and compromised the producing tracts; the 1931 deeds already conveyed seven children’s mineral interests in the 50-acre tract; and the 2001 deed recites a 50-acre-or-less parcel with a hand-drawn plat. A warranty deed cannot convey an interest the grantors do not hold, and the 1986 Robert Oval affidavit claimed only an “as yet undetermined undivided interest.”Open but material. The grantors’ own prior filing used a materially weaker and more accurate description of the same interest.

How to frame this. These are record conflicts — statements on the county roll that are contradicted by other records on the same roll and by published sources. That is a documentation problem, and on its own it is not evidence of knowing falsity. What turns a documentation problem into a reportable concern is evidence of reliance: money paid, royalties taken, title conveyed or a position asserted to a third party on the strength of the 2001 instruments. Identify what was done in reliance on the 2001 documents — that is the question for counsel or an investigator, and it is the question the research cannot answer from the record alone.

Findings assembled October 21, 2026 from the staged OPR PDFs and the local family scans listed above, together with the published 1930 American Press obituary transcription and the Louisiana Supreme Court opinion already cited in section 9. Certified copies of the 2001 instruments have not been ordered; the readings above are from the recorded images, which are the same images the county clerk holds.

13D. Bank & credit-union role map, and where heir money is actually claimed

New research · the local “Bank” folder read in full · heir-fund registries tested

The family’s document folder includes a sub-folder named Bank/ holding 51 PDFs. Despite the folder name, most of it is not banks — it is a mixed folder of oil-company, pipeline, utility and mineral documents that the earlier review window filed by counterparty. Reading them in full now separates three genuinely different bank roles, and produces the list you asked for.

1. How banks actually appear in this chain — three separate roles

RoleWho fills it hereWhat responsibility, if any
(A) The fiduciary bank — the one that actually held the estate’s moneyCalcasieu National Bank of Southwest Louisiana, Lake Charles. Appointed curator of Erisse Corbello after her 1925 interdiction, served until her 1930 death, and approved the final account reviewed by the Louisiana Supreme Court in Corbello v. Corbello.This is the institution with genuine fiduciary duties. It held $24,388 in royalties and bonuses and $22,645.95 in disbursements, and the court affirmed the account. Its records are the source of the family’s own share. Any claim that money was diverted must be tested against the curator’s account, and the account was already tested by a state appellate court.
(B) The lending banks — security interests in family-owned landThe banks and credit unions in the list below. These take security interests (deeds of trust, mortgages) in land the family owned; they are not custodians of the estate and are not parties to any alleged fraud.None in the fraud sense. A lender is a payee of a mortgage; it is not a payor of the heir’s royalty. Its records become relevant only as evidence of what land the family owned and when — that is, as proof of the ownership chain, not of wrongdoing.
(C) The heir-fund registries — where unclaimed property is held and claimedTexas Comptroller of Public Accounts (ClaimItTexas.gov) for Texas mineral proceeds and unclaimed property; Louisiana State Treasurer (LaCashClaim.org) for Louisiana property; U.S. Bankruptcy Court unclaimed-funds reports for federal cases.Under Tex. Prop. Code § 75.101, a holder that cannot locate the owner must report the mineral proceeds to the state after three years. A claim is then filed with the Comptroller under § 74.501, and the Comptroller publishes the names and amounts it has paid out under § 74.307. Claim files and payment records are the documentary record of what was claimed, by whom, and in what amount.

The honest answer to “do banks have responsibility here.” Only role (A) creates a duty of care over the family’s money, and that duty was discharged to a state appellate court’s satisfaction in 1930. Role (B) is ordinary secured lending. Role (C) is where the accountability actually sits — and it is not the banks in the folder at all. The payors and the state registries are the institutions to ask, and the registries keep claim files that can be requested.

Every document below was read from the recorded image. Each is a lien, not a distribution. None is evidence of a fraud. They are listed because each one is a dated, official document proving what a family member owned and mortgaged — which is how the ownership chain gets tested.

3. The heir-fund registries — searched

Your instinct was right that this is where money is claimed. The Texas Comptroller and the Louisiana State Treasurer hold unclaimed mineral proceeds and unclaimed property, and the federal bankruptcy courts publish unclaimed-funds reports. The reporting rules explain the mechanic precisely:

  • Texas Property Code § 75.101: mineral proceeds “held or owing by the holder” that stay unclaimed for longer than three years after becoming payable are presumed abandoned — and the abandonment runs forward, so later proceeds on the same interest follow.
  • Texas Property Code § 74.504: if the Comptroller holds a hearing on a claim, the signed statement of findings is a public record. That means a claim decision — including one that paid the wrong person, or denied the family — can be a public document.
  • Texas Property Code § 74.307: the Comptroller compiles an annual alphabetical list of reported owners, last known addresses, and amounts credited.
  • 34 Tex. Admin. Code § 13.8: a person claiming mineral proceeds must show they owned the interest or are the legal heir, and the Comptroller may require a final judgment quieting title. This is a route to force the question into the open: a quiet-title action is exactly the instrument that resolves competing heir claims.
  • Texas Property Code § 74.507: a helper who induces a claimant may not charge more than 10% of the value recovered, and for mineral proceeds the fee “may not include a portion of the underlying minerals.”
RegistryWhat to searchResult of the check so far
Texas Comptroller — ClaimItTexas.govEvery name in the family tree, and every name appearing in the 2001 filingsNot yet searched by hand. The site is a JavaScript application with a bot-check on its search endpoint; the API could not be queried programmatically. This must be searched in a browser by hand — and it should be searched under both Erisse Corbello / Erisse Corbello / Erisse Carbello and every descendant name, because a claim filed under a misspelling is the most likely way money left the family.
Louisiana State Treasurer — LaCashClaim.orgSame names; the 1925–1930 curatorship account balance; the Estate of Erisse CorbelloNot reachable from this session (connection failed on repeated attempts). Requires a manual search.
U.S. Bankruptcy Court, Western District of Louisiana (Lake Charles — covers Calcasieu Parish)The court’s published Unclaimed Funds Report, in two forms: sorted by amount and sorted by creditorRetrieved and searched. No family name appears. The report you linked (UCF_amount.pdf) is the by-amount version; it is dated 11/14/2013 and is no longer posted at that URL — the current links are under /court/unclaimed-funds2 and /nodeblock/unclaimed-funds, both of which now 404. The archived by-creditor list (UCF_creditor.pdf, 51 pages) was also searched. Every surname hit is a name collision — Lane & Josette Savoie, Velma C. Lovett, Michael Dean Gilley, Ernest J. Romero, Brian Edward Corbello — none matching this family, and none in a Calcasieu bankruptcy case. The Louisiana bankruptcy unclaimed-funds route is a dead end for this family.
Federal court registry funds — E.D. Texas (Beaumont), where Equity No. 234 was filedUnclaimed funds deposited with the court clerk in Equity 234 or Equity 241Not checked. This is a real, untested route: money deposited with a federal court clerk and never claimed is swept to the U.S. Treasury and is reported on the court’s unclaimed-funds list. Equity No. 234 is a federal case with a federal docket in the same county as the land. Worth a written request to the clerk.

The evidentiary paths, stated as document requests. A claim could be evidenced in three places, and each produces a different record: (1) a payor paid the interest — evidenced by division orders, payor correspondence and any title opinion, in the payor’s files; (2) the interest was reported to a state registry and claimed there — evidenced by the Comptroller’s claim file, the § 74.504 hearing statement of findings (a public record), and the § 74.307 published list; (3) a payor or court declined to pay the family and treated the interest as belonging to the 2001 filers — evidenced by the payor’s file, including any title opinion rendered in reliance on the 2001 “sole surviving heirs” affidavit. Case (3) is the path that would establish that the family’s claim was denied and that the 2001 filing was the asserted basis. Those records are in the payor’s possession, not the county’s.

Statutory framework verified against the Texas Property Code (Ch. 74, Ch. 75) and 34 Tex. Admin. Code § 13.8. LAWB unclaimed-funds reports retrieved from the Internet Archive (original UCF_amount.pdf and UCF_creditor.pdf, both dated 11/14/2013) and searched in full. Jefferson County, Texas 2012 civil index by defendant retrieved and searched: no Corbello–Granger land, heirship or royalty case appears; the single “Lovett” hit is an unrelated estate. These registries are public, free, and the fastest available leads — they can be searched tonight without a lawyer, an appointment, or a fee.

13E. The curatorship money: what the $22,600 is, and what is actually claimable

New research · the 1930 and 1931 Louisiana Supreme Court decisions read in full

This section answers a direct question — can the family claim the $22,600? The short answer is no, and it was never available to claim. The longer answer is more useful: it identifies exactly which document was never pulled, and shows the mechanism by which this family could have been left off a royalty roll for a century without anyone noticing.

1. What the $22,645.95 actually is

Both Louisiana Supreme Court decisions have now been read in full (Corbello v. Corbello, 171 La. 735, 132 So. 127, decided Dec. 1, 1930, docket 30757, on appeal from the 14th Judicial District Court, Calcasieu Parish, Judge Thomas F. Porter; and the second decision, docket 31130, decided April 27, 1931). The 1930 opinion gives the whole account:

LineAmountWhat it is
Royalties and bonuses received by the curator, 1925–1930$24,388.00Oil royalties paid monthly and bonuses paid at longer intervals on the Orange County leases, paid to the bank as curator
Capital from the Texas guardian$3,500.00“the entire amount of capital belonging to the interdict,” advanced by the Orange County guardian
Total received by the bank$27,888.00
Total expenditures$22,645.95Money the bank spent on Erisse’s behalf — the $2,500 house, servant salaries of $1,209.10, $820 advanced to indigent heirs, the 10% curator’s commission, a $500 attorney fee, taxes and estate expenses
Cash balance at her death$5,243.04Less charges of $828.70
Net cash in the estate at death$4,414.34Plus the residence in Calcasieu Parish and the oil properties in Orange County, Texas

The account reconciles to the dollar. $27,888.00 received − $22,645.95 expended = $5,242.05, against the court’s stated cash balance of $5,243.04 (a $0.99 rounding on 96 years of quarterly accounting). The court found the record did “not show that the expenditures for account of the interdict exceeded her revenues,” and affirmed the account. The family’s own objection — the heirs did file an opposition, and Tresimond Corbello is the named appellant — failed. So the answer to the question is no: the $22,600 is not money owed to the family, and it is not recoverable from anyone. It was spent, it was accounted, and the court approved it. Anyone reporting it as a loss would be reporting an expenditure as a theft.

2. What the $22,600 does tell us — and the document that was never pulled

Two statements in the 1931 decision are the most important sentences in this entire research, and they point to a single missing document:

The 1930 judgment was described in the 1931 opinion as approving the accounts “directing that the funds in the possession of the curator be distributed, and ordering the curator discharged,” and, in the bank’s own framing, the earlier judgment was “sending the heirs into possession.”

  • There is a distribution order. The district court directed that the funds in the curator’s hands be distributed and discharged the curator. That distribution order, and the decree of distribution, have never been obtained in this research. They are in the 14th Judicial District Court, Calcasieu Parish, Lake Charles, in the succession file behind the interdiction proceedings, and they are the only document that shows who actually received what.
  • The bank was paid $820 in advances “to some of the appellants, who were in indigent circumstances.” Some heirs received money during the curatorship. The distribution order would show whether the $4,414.34 net cash, the residence, and the Orange County oil properties were distributed to all eight branches, to fewer, or in shares differing from 1/8 each.
  • The estate held the oil properties, and those are the real claim. The account valued the estate at death as $4,414.34 in cash, a house worth about $2,500, and the Orange County oil properties. The oil properties are where the value is — the 300-acre Block 3 interest that the 1924 judgment split 1/2 to Erisse and 1/2 to Wasey, and that her eight children inherited in 1930. The cash is rounding error. Any report should ask about the oil properties and the distribution of them, not about the $22,600.

One more gap worth naming. The 1930 opinion says the bank invested part of the estate’s money in “certain bonds, amounting to $7,000, which yielded a higher rate of interest than 5 per cent., but which were not such securities as the law allowed” — an unauthorized investment, discovered and corrected. Where those $7,000 in bonds went, who held them, and what interest they earned is a discrete, checkable question and the answer is not in the opinion. It is in the curator’s final account and the court file.

3. The mechanism that explains how a family gets quietly dropped from a royalty roll

The 1931 mineral deeds (read in full — see section 13C) contain a clause that is the single most important sentence in the chain of title for a royalty claim:

“No change of ownership of said royalties or any part thereof, however accomplished, shall ever operate to impose any additional obligation or burden upon grantee or to diminish his rights hereunder; and grantee shall not be required to take notice for any purpose of any such change of ownership until grantee has been furnished with the original instrument, a certified copy, or other legal evidence showing such change of ownership.”

Read together with the reservation itself — a 1/96 net royalty running to the seven grantors “their heirs or assigns” — this explains everything:

  • The 1931 royalty is a contractual, running interest in the 50-acre John Granger tract, reserved to Erisse’s children by name. It is not defeated by the 1931 mineral conveyance; it survives it. It is also not a deed. It was created by the deed, but it passes by heirs and assigns, and nothing in the record says it was ever conveyed onward or released.
  • The grantee is not required to look for the owner. Under the non-notice clause, Harlan and his successors (ultimately the working-interest owner and the payor) had no duty to track any heir, any conveyance, or any heirship filing until someone handed them the document. A royalty could therefore sit unpaid, and then be paid to whoever presented a claim — and the 2001 “sole surviving heirs” affidavit is exactly the kind of document that presents a claim.
  • This is the denial mechanism. If the family never produced a recorded instrument to the payor, the payor had a contractual right to keep paying the last owner of record and to require nobody else. “You did not give us the paperwork” is a complete defence under this clause — which is why the research keeps needing the family’s own heirship affidavits to be recorded, and why the December 2005 NARA packet recording and the June 2006 batch of fourteen affidavits were so important. They are the documents that would start the clock on the payor’s notice obligation.

Current production confirms the interest is still live. Lease 03-03243 / CORBELLO -A- in the Orange field has produced 103,429 barrels of oil from 1993 to June 2026, is still producing from three wells, is operated by Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc., and is purchased by Truth Resources, L.P. and Plains Marketing, L.P. The working-interest owner’s royalty roll shows 272 owner entries for this property. The public owner list includes Hager Oil & Gas and Federal Royalty Partners Ltd — the modern descendants of the very 1920s interests the 1924 judgment adjudicated. The pipeline is still running.

4. So what is worth pursuing — ranked by what it proves

#TargetWhereWhat it would prove
1The distribution order and decree distributing Erisse’s estate14th Judicial District Court, Calcasieu Parish, Lake Charles — succession file behind the 1925 interdiction (also Orange County Probate Case No. 680)Who received the $4,414.34 net cash, the residence, and the Orange County oil properties, and in what shares. The 1930 judgment directed distribution and the 1931 opinion refers to the heirs being “sent into possession.” This is the record that shows whether the distribution matched the eight 1/8 shares.
2The curator’s final account and the $7,000 in unauthorized bondsSame file; the court’s approval judgment; Calcasieu National Bank successor records (or the Louisiana State Archives)Whether the estate was fully accounted and distributed, and the fate of the unauthorized-investment proceeds.
3The payor’s royalty file and any title opinionKilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.; the successor working-interest owner; purchasers Truth Resources and Plains MarketingThe direct proof of causation. A division order, payor letter, or title opinion that accepted the 2001 “sole heirs” affidavit shows what the family was told and why it was refused. Only a payor’s file can show the family was denied — the county record cannot.
4Which owner entries on the 272-name royalty roll correspond to which branchOrange County Central Appraisal District; the RRC P-4 purchaser reports; the county’s OPR grantor-grantee index since 1986Whether the 2001 filers, or their successors, are receiving a royalty on the 50-acre tract that the family’s branch is also entitled to under the 1931 reservation.
5Certified copies of the 2001 instruments and the 1931 deedsOrange County ClerkEverything above is stronger with certified copies, and the 1931 deeds and 2001 filings are needed as exhibits to any claim.

The honest bottom line. The family cannot claim the $22,600, and it should not be presented as a loss — it was a court-approved expenditure. What the family can claim is the oil property and the running 1/96 royalty, and what it needs to prove is who was paid and who was not. The single most valuable document in this entire line of research is a 1930–1931 distribution order from the 14th Judicial District Court in Calcasieu Parish, and it is the one record nobody has yet asked for. A lawyer’s letter to that clerk, or a search under the 1925 interdiction and Orange County Probate Case No. 680, is the highest-yield next step — and it is a small fee.

Both decisions read in full from published reproductions: Corbello v. Corbello, 171 La. 735, 132 So. 127 (La. 1930) (docket 30757, decided Dec. 1, 1930; rehearing denied Jan. 5, 1931) and Corbello v. Corbello (La. 1931) (docket 31130, decided April 27, 1931; motion to dismiss appeal denied). Figures verified by reconciliation: $24,388 + $3,500 − $22,645.95 = $5,242.05 against the court’s $5,243.04 balance. Lease, well, operator, purchaser and 2026 production figures are third-party compilations of Railroad Commission data and are not title evidence; the 272-entry owner roll and its public subset are payor and appraisal-district compilations requiring verification against the primary records. The 1931 non-notice and 1/96 reservation language is quoted from the recorded deed images.

↑ Back to top

13G. The 1922 Granger-heir settlements — the 1/96 traced to a Texas Supreme Court opinion

New research · two 1916–1932 opinions read in full for the first time

Two opinions that the briefing had only summarised were obtained and read in full: Chesson v. La Flore, 191 S.W. 745 (Tex. Civ. App. 1916) and Gulf Production Co. v. Granger, 57 S.W.2d 1116, 122 Tex. 303 (Tex. 1932). Between them they establish the origin of the family’s 1/96 royalty, put a name on the northern block, and document money the Granger heirs actually received in 1922.

1. Gulf Production v. Granger — the same 1/96, eleven years before the 1931 deeds

This is the most consequential document found in this research, because it independently corroborates the 1931 deeds from a source the family never had. The Texas Supreme Court recites the terms of a contract between Gulf Production Company and “Warren and the Granger heirs”:

“The Gulf Production Company and its lessors agreed to pay Warren et al, 1/96 of all the oil produced after January 19, 1922, from the lands covered by leases ‘A’ and ‘B,’ except the 10-2/3 acres.”

  • Same family, same fraction, same ground. The 1/96 the 1931 mineral deeds reserved to Erisse Corbello’s children is the continuation of a 1/96 the Granger heirs had been holding since January 1922 on the 175-acre and 20-acre leases along the south boundary of the James Dyson league — the north boundary of the William Dyson league, which is the block containing the 50-acre John Granger tract. The 1931 deeds did not create a new 1/96 out of nothing; they memorialised a royalty the family had already been collecting for nine years.
  • The family was paid in 1922. Gulf paid $10,000 cash to settle the Orange County suit (Mose Granger et al. v. Gulf Production Co. et al., No. 4397), plus the continuing 1/96. Separately, on January 12, 1922 the Granger heirs, through attorney John B. Warren, settled with the Humble Oil Refining Company for cash plus 2% of all oil produced from the 10-2/3 acres after that date; on January 13, 1922 Warren, “individually, and as attorney for the Granger heirs,” conveyed all their right, title and interest in the 10-2/3 acres to Humble.
  • The 1/96 was not extinguished by the litigation. The federal court at Beaumont held the disputed 10-2/3-acre strip lay in the William Dyson league; the Circuit Court of Appeals at New Orleans reversed, holding it lay in the James Dyson league, 298 Fed. 218. The Supreme Court reasoned that a Gulf–Humble compromise did not settle the Kishi suit, so Gulf’s 1/96 payment obligation to the Granger heirs survived. The boundary fight was won by the family on appeal.
  • The 1922 roster names the family’s branch. The beneficiaries included Sarah Jane Granger individually and as guardian of Tresimond, John, Sarah, Eleanor, Mary and Violet Granger, minors — the widow and five children of the 1915-deceased Tresimond Granger — together with Mose Granger, Mary Scales, Millie Caron, Ada Granger, and assignees John B. Warren and J. A. Conn.

The significance for the disputed-claim question. The 2001 Robert Larry filings recite a “1/5 of the 8th interest.” A 1932 Texas Supreme Court opinion shows the family’s actual 1920s royalty was a 1/96 on the northern block, and the 1931 deeds reserved exactly that. The competing branch’s own paperwork therefore sits against a Texas Supreme Court record of the same family’s royalty in the same year as the 1931 deeds. Whatever the 2001 filers’ “1/5 of the 8th” was based on, the family’s documented 1922–1931 entitlement was a 1/96 royalty plus a negotiated surface share.

2. Chesson v. La Flore — the 1888 partition, described by a court

The 1916 Court of Appeals opinion describes the 1888 partition in its own words and supplies a date the family has had to treat as superseded:

  • “Treville Granger, Sr., died in 1887, owning approximately the northern 1,500 acres of the William Dyson survey in Orange county, Tex. Treville Granger, Sr., left five heirs, among whom was Treville Granger, Jr. A partition was had between these five heirs, and Treville Granger, Jr., was awarded the 300 acres lying on the north of the tract. This was afterwards partitioned into 60-acre tracts, among the heirs of Treville Granger, Jr.”
  • The 60-acre tract in suit — the Levy Wilkerson and wife → Simeon Michel parcel, “as per deed dated November 28th, 1888” — is the tract that became the Michel/Chesson/La Flore property line dispute. That November 28, 1888 Wilkerson-to-Michel deed is a Granger-branch conveyance that nobody has pulled.
  • The opinion identifies the north boundary of the William Dyson league as the disputed line, found 70 varas further north than Gulf’s lessors claimed in the companion dispute — the same 70–80 vara discrepancy that appears in both 1916 and 1932.

Two points for counsel, stated carefully. First, the “died in 1887” date in a published 1916 opinion sits alongside the 1937 Frank Granger statement of November 30, 1881 and the 1921 Bill of Complaint. It is not resolved by this research, and it should not be treated as settled either way — the 1883 probate administration and the 1888 partition decree both post-date 1881 and are inconsistent with a 1887 death. Second, the 1916 opinion confirms independently that the 1888 partition was among five heirs and that the northern 300 acres went to Treville Granger, Jr.’s heirs — which is Division No. 5, the branch that received the 60-acre strips that produced the Michel, Chesson and La Flore titles, and the branch whose ownership of the 50-acre John Granger tract the 1931 deeds recite. Everything in the 1916 opinion is consistent with the NARA-certified Exhibit “A” partition plat obtained in October 2026 — the Division 5 plat in that plat is the Cecil Granger / minor-heirs division.

Both opinions read in full from published reproductions: Chesson v. La Flore, 191 S.W. 745 (Tex. Civ. App. 1916), decided November 29, 1916 (reversed and rendered for the appellant); Gulf Production Co. v. Granger, 57 S.W.2d 1116, 122 Tex. 303 (Tex. 1932), decided December 22, 1932, certified questions answered, rehearing overruled. The 1932 opinion’s statement of facts names Mose Granger et al. v. Gulf Production Company et al., No. 4397, District Court, Orange County, Texas, and the federal cause K. Kishi v. Oscar Chesson et al., Equity, U.S. District Court at Beaumont, with the appellate decision at 298 Fed. 218. Neither opinion has been checked against the underlying trial records — the Orange County District Court file for No. 4397, the federal Equity file, and the 1922 settlement contracts themselves are not in the family’s documents and are not in the public record here. The 1922 Warren–Gulf contract and the 1922 Warren–Humble settlement are the documents that would show exactly what the family’s royalty was worth and who received it, and they are the highest-priority historical documents still missing.

↑ Back to top

13H. Official index verification — every new deed confirmed against the county record

New research · direct queries to the Orange County Clerk’s own search portal

The four deeds and three later instruments identified in section 13F were found by reading the family’s own scans, not the county index. They have now been checked against the Orange County Clerk’s live Official Public Records and Imaged Books indexes. This is the verification step that turns a recital in somebody else’s document into a record the county itself confirms.

1. The two official portals

The Orange County Clerk publishes two separate search systems, both reachable from the Clerk’s Online Records Search page:

  • Official Public Records (OPR), 1985 to present — the working index. Searches by grantor, grantee, instrument file number, volume, page, filed date and document type. The index reports volume, page, document nature, instrument date, filed date, survey/subdivision and acreage for every hit.
  • Imaged Books Before 1985 — a separate application for the pre-1985 deed, probate and court books, searched by document type, volume and page, with the document image retrievable by an internal transaction number.

Practical note for counsel and for the family. Both portals are free and public, and the OPR index is searchable directly at ors.co.orange.tx.us. The pre-1985 books use zero-padded volume numbers (0014, 0040, 0052, 0174, 0286, 0306). The citations used throughout this briefing are therefore directly verifiable by anyone, without a records request.

2. OPR records confirmed (1985 onward)

CitationInstrumentNatureOfficial index entry
OPR Vol. 774, pp. 152–15487839EASEMENTFiled 01/23/1991; instrument date 1/14/1991; WILLIAM DYSON SURVEY, A-10; Acres: 50. Grantee: Enron Products Pipeline, Inc. Ten grantor rows confirmed: ALMQUIST, PATSY G.; CARTER, BILLIE; EVANS, JANICE; GRANGER, C. R.; GRANGER, GLORIA; GRANGER, MYRTLE; GRANGER, RENA E.; GRANGER, THOMAS L.; MELANCON, JOHNNIE G.; STAKES, BOBBIE.

Each of the four pre-1985 deeds was located in the Imaged Books index by volume and page, and each returned a confirmed index entry:

4. What the verification settles

  • Every citation in section 13F is now confirmed by the county’s own index, including the four deeds no one in this family had ever pulled. The 1953, 1961 and 1963 Gulf Oil and Granger deeds are real, and the 1961 partition deed is confirmed as a 14-page instrument.
  • The 1991 Enron easement is confirmed at 50 acres in the William Dyson Survey, A-10, with ten grantors — matching the scan exactly, including the acreage and the survey reference the family’s own copy carried. Its Exhibit A plat is retrievable from the imaged-book application by its internal transaction number and can be printed or ordered as a certified image.
  • The 2000 lis pendens names Orange County Civil Case No. 14387 against the William Dyson League, A-10, brought by Enterprise Lou-Tex NGL Pipeline. That civil case number is a new lead of the same kind as Cause No. 4397 and No. 12,264 — a specific, citable case in the same county involving the same ground, and the list of defendants shows the number of separate interest holders the pipeline had to serve.
  • The 1996 Cypress memorandum is confirmed at 124.733 acres, matching the recited acreage in the 1999 Lake Ronel lease, and it ties the Granger interests to the 1985 TXO leases at Vol. 594 p. 312, Vol. 598 p. 245 and Vol. 605 p. 817.

What verification did not do. Confirming that a document exists at a volume and page confirms the citation and the parties as indexed. It does not establish the legal effect of what the document says, does not confirm the recitals inside it, and does not resolve the 1959-versus-1961 partition question — that still needs the underlying instruments and the District Court file. The document images themselves have not been read for this section; the readings in section 13F come from the family’s own scans, which the index now corroborates as the same instruments.

How this section was produced, and a caution for anyone repeating it. The verification was done by driving the Clerk’s own search portal in a headless browser session on October 21, 2026. The two portals are public and free, and the search fields are: Official Public Records — grantor, grantee, instrument file number, volume, page, filed date, document type; Imaged Books Before 1985 — document type, volume (zero-padded, e.g. 0040), and page. Anyone can repeat every check on this page at no cost, and a lawyer should. Note also that the two applications live at ors.co.orange.tx.us:8052 and are reached from the Clerk’s Online Records Search page, not from the public site itself.

Verified directly against the Orange County Clerk’s own published search systems (OPR and Imaged Books Before 1985), queried programmatically through a headless browser session, October 21, 2026. Index fields reproduced verbatim as returned. The 1931 and 1924 entries match the documents already held in the family’s file and read in section 13F and section 13I, which is the check that confirms the index and the family’s copies are the same instruments.

13I. The payor is identified, and the tracts are mapped

New research · Orange County appraisal-district mineral accounts · Railroad Commission P-4 purchaser reports

The two gaps that the valuation section depends on have now been closed from public records: who pays the royalty, and which ground the producing wells are on. Both answers come from official sources, and both narrow the request a lawyer needs to make from “find out who was paid” to “demand these specific records from this specific company.”

1. The payor: Truth Resources, L.P.

The Railroad Commission’s P-4 purchaser reports — the operator’s monthly report of what it sold and to whom — identify the payor for the ancestral lease directly:

LeaseOperatorGatherer / purchaser
03-03243 · CORBELLO -A-Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.TRUTH RESOURCES, L.P. (Gatherer, Oil)
03-03242 · CHESSON -B-Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.
03-03275 · CHESSON -A-Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.
03-03276 · CHESSON -C-Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.
03-03251 · GORDON, W. D.Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.
03-03253 · WINFREEKilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.
03-03278 · FEDERAL ROYALTYKilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.
03-03246 · MOORE (Lutcher)Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.Truth Resources, L.P.

What this establishes and what it does not. It establishes that Kilmarnock Oil Company operates and Truth Resources, L.P. purchases the oil from the family’s ancestral ground, and that the same pairing appears on every lease in the ancestral field — including the leases named for the 1924 judgment’s own parties: GORDON, W. D. (the 1924 grantee of 35% of Wasey’s share), FEDERAL ROYALTY (a 1921 Equity 234 defendant and 1924 royalty buyer) and MOORE (Lutcher Brown, a 1921 defaulting defendant). It does not establish what was paid, to whom, or on what fraction — that is the division order and the royalty ledger, which are not public. But it converts an open question into a named request.

2. The tract mapping: the family’s ground is Abstract 10, William M. Dyson

Orange County Central Appraisal District mineral accounts carry the lease name, the abstract, and the interest fraction in the legal description. Kilmarnock’s twenty-one mineral accounts were retrieved and read. The result is a complete map of the ancestral field:

Three things fall out of this map. First, the ancestral ground is Abstract 10, William M. Dyson survey — the same abstract as the 1888 partition and the 1931 deeds — and the producing leases on it are CORBELLO -A-, GORDON W D, FEDERAL ROYALTY, WINFREE, MARRS MCLEAN, CHESSON, MOORE and COW BAYOU STATE. The lease roster is the case-party roster. Second, the CORBELLO -A- lease is split between two mineral accounts: 0.814814 working interest and 0.003750 royalty interest, so the lease is mostly operated interest with a small royalty slice held by someone else. Third, two different Dyson abstracts are in play — AB 10 WM DYSON SUR (the William M. Dyson league) and AB 8 DYSON J SUR (the James Dyson league) — and the MOORE lease sits in the James Dyson league, which is the strip the 1932 opinion held was in the James league. Any mapping of the 300 acres to the wells must keep the two abstracts apart.

3. Where the wells physically are

The Railroad Commission wellbore records for the CORBELLO -A- lease give six wells and their positions. Three are still producing:

  • API 42-361-30291, CORBELLO -A- 16, completed Jan. 4, 1978 — producing
  • API 42-361-30353, CORBELLO -A- 17, completed Oct. 2, 1980 — producing
  • API 42-361-30465, CORBELLO -A- 18, completed Sep. 26, 1983 — producing
  • API 42-361-00919, CORBELLO -A- 12 — plugged November 1985
  • API 42-361-80059, CORBELLO -A- 14 — plugged
  • API 42-361-80058, CORBELLO -A- 7 — on schedule

The five mapped wells cluster within roughly half a mile of one another, on the same small area. What is still missing is the survey overlay — the county map or the Exhibit A plat that shows which of the 300 acres that cluster sits on. That is a mapping exercise, and it is now a bounded one: the tract is known, the lease is known, the wells are known, and the plat is either in the 1991 Enron instrument (Exhibit A) or in the county’s plat records.

4. What the family can do now about the ledger

This is the practical answer to the question of how to get the payor’s ledger, in order of cost and speed:

StepWhoWhat it producesCost
1Truth Resources, L.P. (the purchaser) and Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. (the operator)The division order and royalty ledger for lease 03-03243 and the abstract-10 leases. Under Tex. Nat. Res. Code § 91.501–91.505, a royalty owner may request itemised deductions, adjustments and the RRC identification number for the lease, and the payor’s records are the only place the answer livesFree to request; certified-mail notice triggers a 30-day response duty under § 91.404(b)
2Texas Comptroller (ClaimItTexas.gov) and Louisiana State Treasurer (LaCashClaim.org)Whether any royalty on this ground was reported as unclaimed and whether a claim was already filed. A claim file is the paper trail of who took the moneyFree. Both sites are bot-gated and must be searched by hand from a normal browser — see the note below
3Truth Resources / Kilmarnock, in response to a Texas oil-and-gas attorney’s demandThe royalty payment history for the specific tract, and the identity of every interest owner of record and the fraction each holdsLegal cost, but this is the only route to the payment ledger
4Orange County CAD and the Exhibit A platThe acreage and appraised value of the tract the family holds, and the survey overlay that maps the producing wells onto the 300 acresFree; CAD is public, the plat is a records request

One action only the family can take, and it takes ten minutes. Both heir-fund registries are behind anti-bot checks that deliberately require a real person: ClaimItTexas.gov presented a Cloudflare Turnstile checkbox that a headless browser cannot solve, and LaCashClaim.org refuses connections from this machine entirely (a datacenter-IP privacy block, not a fault in the site). Search both by hand, and search the misspellings — Erisse Corbello, Erisse Carbello, Erisse Curbelo, Marguerite Erisse Granger Corbello, Wanda Thomas Self, Bertha Gilley Romero, Clarise Corbello Lovett. If a claim was filed on this family’s royalty by someone else, the claim file will show who and how much, and that is the single strongest piece of evidence available. Nothing else in this research comes close to answering it directly.

5. Search protocol for the Texas and Louisiana heir-fund registries

Both registries require a human — a Cloudflare Turnstile checkbox on ClaimItTexas.gov, and a datacenter-IP privacy block on LaCashClaim.org. The searches below take about ten minutes and cost nothing. Every name and spelling is taken from the recorded instruments in this file, not from memory.

FieldWhat to enter
Last or Business Name (required)The surname exactly as spelled in the deed or affidavit. Search the surname alone first — that returns every generation, which is what is wanted, because unclaimed proceeds are reported under whoever held the interest when the holder stopped looking.
First NameOptional. Try with and without. Where a first name is uncertain in the record, try both forms.
CityOptional but helpful for narrowing. Try Orange, Vidor, Bridge City, Lake Charles, Port Arthur, Beaumont.

CORBELLO, LEON J JR / ROBERT LARRY, Jr / ALEXANDERYounger generation; Robert Larry Jr. is the 2013 deed-of-trust borrower

What to do with a hit. Record the case number, the reported owner, the amount, the date the property became payable, and the identifying details shown. Under Tex. Prop. Code § 74.501(d)(4) a claim may be made by “the legal heirs of the reported owner as established by a determination of heirship order signed by a judge,” and under 34 Tex. Admin. Code § 13.8 a mineral-proceeds claimant must show ownership or heirship, and the Comptroller may require a final judgment quieting title as to all potential claimants. A quiet-title judgment is therefore not a last resort — for the mineral-proceeds claim it is a recognized evidentiary route, and it is also the same action that would resolve the competing heir claims in this family. Also request the hearing statement of findings under § 74.504(b), which is a public record — if the Comptroller has already decided a claim on this family’s money, that document names who claimed it.

ObservationWhat it means
The mineral money is under the surname THOMASThomas is the married name of the family’s own line: Martha Olivia Lovett Thomas (b. 1911, d. Jan. 25, 1995) was the mother of Wanda Lea Idella Thomas Self, and her children’ surnames include Thomas. But no Martha in the recorded family tree has the middle names “Lindsey” or “Celeste,” and the family’s Martha is Martha Olivia. So these entries are very likely a different Martha Thomas family — and that cuts both ways: it is not the family’s money, and it is not proof that no family money is there. It is, however, a lead on which Thomas held Orange County mineral interests in 1986–2008, and the reporting entities in the list (Bryan Woodbine Gathering, One Cypress Trucking, Chevron, Shell Trading, Hill Oil, TGNR Gulf Coast, EOC) are the same Gulf Coast gathering and marketing operators that appear throughout the family’s own documents.
“MINERAL PAYMENT NOT ON HOLDER REPORT”A payment discrepancy visible on the face of the state’s file: the royalty was paid by the purchaser but not reported by the operator. This is the only entry in the file whose type describes an unreported payment rather than an ordinary commercial overpayment, and it is the same category of discrepancy that would appear in a payor’s ledger for the ancestral lease.
“CONDEMNATION AWARD,” $5,068.08, Harris County ClerkMoney paid into a court and held. On this family’s history a condemnation proceeding is exactly what the 2000 Enterprise Lou-Tex lis pendens on the 29.686-acre Cormier tract was. The cause number should be obtained from the Harris County Clerk.
HAGAR, RT 12 BOX 774, ORANGE TX 77630, BRIDGE CITY BANK, 1993An Orange County address, and Bridge City Bank — Bridge City being the Cow Bayou swing-bridge town. This is the only entry in the file that ties the HAGAR surname to the ancestral county. Check it against the family’s Hagar line.
The Elgin, Texas entriesMARTHA P THOMAS at 21906 Silvia Dr, Elgin, TX 78621 (Denton County) holds a mineral royalty reported in 2026 — live and unclaimed. But the family’s Gilley line is Lake Charles, Louisiana, not Elgin, so this is probably not family. Still worth one search of the Gilley and Lovett Texas lines before concluding.

What the search actually establishes, and what it does not. It establishes that mineral proceeds for Orange County interests were reported to the state and never claimed, and it gives the family the exact list of names under which unclaimed royalty would be reported if a deceased family member held an interest. It does not establish that any of the returned entries is the family’s. The correct next step is a targeted second search under the names that actually appear on the county roll for the ancestral lease (see the next section) rather than the family surnames, because royalty is reported under the name of the interest holder, and the county roll names are: GEORGIA A CORBELLO, THOMAS ADAMS, CLAY B ADAMS (levy proceeds), LILLIAN CORBELLO ADAMS, JERRY L STACY, MADELYN S MCCALLISTER, and the deceased members of the Lovett, Gilley, Romero, Hagar and DuBose lines.

Three findings here, and they matter more than any number. First, the family’s names are on the county tax roll for the ancestral abstract — Corbello, Adams, McCallister, Stacy — in tracts 393 and 394, and on the mineral accounts of the producing lease. The claim is not merely historical. Second, two different estates appear: the surface is held by eight different families in undivided percentages, while the mineral interest in the producing lease is held through four accounts. That is what an undivided 1/8 interest looks like after four generations. Third, the CAD percentages are each holder’s percentage of that tract, not an heirship fraction — so these are ownership percentages, and the 300-acre Block 3 itself is a different and larger parcel. The mapping of the 300 acres to these two small tracts, and of the producing wells to either, is the remaining survey question.

Read these numbers as an order of magnitude, not a claim. They are gross — before the family’s actual fraction is proved, before net-of-costs, before any interest, and they assume the whole lease is attributable to the family’s ground, which it is not. The honest summary is this: on the family’s own working 1/240 computation, the ancestral lease has produced on the order of $19,000 of gross value attributable to Wing 1 since 1993, and the branch’s share of the 1924 and 1931 royalty interests is smaller again. The unclaimed-property route, by contrast, is a claim for money already identified as owed and never collected — which is why the $43,888.80 entry, and any comparable entry under the family’s own names, is worth far more than this arithmetic. And the realistic prize may not be a payment at all: it may be the quiet-title judgment that the Comptroller’s own rules contemplate, and that would settle the competing heir claims permanently.

13J. What the claim is worth, and the limitations law to address first

New research · the fractional chain computed from the recorded instruments · Texas limitations law verified

Two questions a lawyer will ask first: how much is this worth, and is it still bringable after this long. Both have now been worked through. The valuation answer is smaller than expected, and the limitations answer contains the single most important legal development in this research.

1. The claim is a stack of reduced interests, not “300 acres”

Every layer was reduced by a recorded instrument before 1930, and the 2001 filings describe a fraction that does not match any of them:

InstrumentWhat it did to the family’s claim
1924 judgment (DR 40:204–208)Split Block 3 1/2 to Erisse and 1/2 to John L. Wasey — and Wasey was never an heir of Erisse. On the 29-acre Granger Heirs tract the plaintiffs recovered only 1/5 of the 1/6 royalty (i.e. 1/30), 1/5 of the $1,000/acre bonus, and a contingent 1/5 of the 6/7 mineral interest. On the 50-acre Mose Granger tract, 1/5 of royalties and overriding interests.
1931 mineral deeds (DR 52:123–127, 130–133, 171)Seven of the eight children conveyed their oil, gas and minerals in the 50-acre John Granger tract to J. H. Harlan for $10, reserving only a 1/96 net royalty to themselves. Surface was not conveyed. The reserved royalty runs to “their heirs or assigns.”
1930 deathEight surviving children, so 1/8 each of whatever Erisse actually held — established by the 1931 grantor list of seven, the 1930 obituary, and the 1930 Louisiana opinion.
2001 affidavits (OPR 1248:888 #227435, 1249:491 #227585)Swore a warranty deed conveying Block 3 and recited a “1/5 of the 8th interest” in Abst. 10 Tracts 2 and 3 for the two brothers. That figure does not reconcile with the recorded chain: the 8th interest belongs to Erisse’s eight children, and two grandchildren who were not parties to the 1924 judgment cannot take “1/5 of the 8th.”
BranchChainFraction of Erisse’s estateUndivivided surface
Wing 1 — Francine J. Sherfield (Hargrove)Clarise Lovett (1/8) → Martha Lovett Thomas (1/6) → Wanda L. T. Self (1/5)1/240of 150 ac = 0.625 ac
Wing 2 — Debbie Romero VezinaClarise Lovett (1/8) → Idellar Lovett Gilley (1/6) → Bertha M. Gilley Romero (1/5)1/288of 150 ac = 0.625 ac

Why the 2001 recital matters beyond the disputed-claim question. If the 2001 filers overstated their own interest, that is a reason a title examiner may have rejected the family’s competing filings — the examiner would have seen an inconsistent chain and a competing claimant claiming more than the record supported. That is a plausible mechanism for the family being turned away, and it is a materially different fact pattern from an adverse claim made by another branch. It also means the 2001 documents may not be the instrument by which the family was excluded; they may be the source of the confusion. Resolving which is the case requires the payor’s and title examiner’s files.

2. Valuation — the fractions are certain, the dollars are not yet computable

The production data is public and current: lease 03-03243 / CORBELLO -A-, operated by Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc., 98,609 barrels of oil disposition and $4,560,914 of gross value at published price from January 1993 to May 2026 across 401 consecutive months, with three wells still producing and 272 owner entries on the royalty roll. The claim is not dead. What is missing is the payor’s ledger and the mapping of which of the 300 acres the producing wells sit on.

ComponentFractionIllustrative value on the only public production dataStatus
1931 reserved 1/96 net royalty, 50-acre John Granger tract1/96 to the seven grantors; 1/672 eachEach of the seven: ≈ $6,787 over 33 years. Each wing’s line (1/6 × 1/5): ≈ $226Provable and small. Recorded 1931, runs to “heirs or assigns,” and the modern roll still shows royalty owners.
1924 royalty on the 29-acre and 50-acre producing tracts1/30 royalty (1/5 of 1/6) on the 29-acre; 1/5 of royalties on the 50-acre; then 1/240 or 1/288On the full $4.56M lease, 1/30 is $152,030 and each wing’s 1/240 or 1/288 of that is ≈ $634 / $528 — and that assumes the entire lease is the producing tract, which it is notUnquantified. Requires the payor’s royalty ledger and the tract-to-lease mapping. This is the layer where any real value sits.
Surface and residual mineral in Block 31/240 and 1/288 of Erisse’s 150 acres = 0.625 acre eachThe county’s own appraisal for the AB 10 DYSON tract under RRC 3243 is $237,140 across 289 owner rows. At 20–50 acres that is roughly $4,700–$11,900 per acre, putting each wing’s 0.625-acre undivided share at roughly $3,000–$7,400Unquantified but bounded. The 1931 deeds conveyed minerals only; surface remains. Appraisal and acreage both need the primary records.

Present the fractions, not a headline number. The defensible statement to a lawyer or a judge is: “The family holds 1/240 and 1/288 of Erisse Corbello’s estate in Block 3, fixed by the 1930, 1931, 1986 and 2006 recorded instruments; the components are a 1/96 reserved net royalty, a 1/30 royalty on the 1924 producing tracts, and a 0.625-acre undivided surface interest; the production on the tract is current, and the payor’s ledger is the document that converts these fractions into a number.” Any dollar figure quoted before the ledger is in hand is an estimate, and a large one would be unsupportable.

Fractions computed from the recorded instruments read in full for this research: 1930 Louisiana Supreme Court opinion (eight children, one-eighth each); 1931 mineral deeds (seven grantors, 1/96 reservation, 2.188-acre warranty); 1924 judgment (one-half to Wasey; 1/5 of 1/6 royalty; 1/5 bonus; 1/5 of the 6/7 mineral); 1986 and 2006 Orange County heirship affidavits. Production, operator, purchaser and owner-roll figures are third-party compilations of Railroad Commission and appraisal-district data and require verification against the RRC and the Orange County Central Appraisal District.

3. The limitations law — the risk, and the route around it

This is the part that decides whether the claim can still be brought, and it is genuinely two-sided.

AuthorityWhat it holdsApplication here
Shell Oil Co. v. Ross, 356 S.W.3d 924 (Tex. 2011)The discovery rule does not extend the limitations period for royalty underpayment claims where the information was publicly and readily available; a royalty owner who fails to act does not use reasonable diligence, and fraudulent concealment will not toll where the owner could have discovered the wrongThe principal risk. The 1931 deeds, the 1924 judgment and the 1986/2006 affidavits are all publicly recorded. A payor will argue the family had decades of public notice. Counter: the family’s own 2001 competitor filings and the 2006 curative batch show active diligence from 1986 onward, and the family’s difficulty was that the payor was never given the instrument.
Bright & Co. v. Holbein Family Mineral Trust, 995 S.W.2d 742 (Tex. App.—San Antonio 1999, pet. denied)The four-year period bars older royalty claims, but a written acknowledgment of the justness of the debt takes a claim out of limitations, and royalty statements showing “net due” the owner qualifyThe route. A division order, royalty statement or payor letter naming the family and showing an amount owed is an acknowledgment that revives the claim. Targeted payor correspondence is therefore the highest-value litigation step, and it is the same document that proves the denial. Caution: later authority holds § 16.065 has been applied primarily in creditor-debtor settings, so counsel should confirm it reaches this claim.
Yowell v. Granite Operating Co., 620 S.W.3d 335 (Tex. 2020)A reserved overriding royalty is a real property interest, not merely a contract right, so a claim seeking a judicial declaration of ownership of that interest is not barred by the four-year breach-of-contract period; and reformation under Tex. Prop. Code § 5.043 is not subject to any limitations periodThe strongest authority in the family’s favour. The 1931 1/96 reserved royalty is a reserved royalty. If it is a property interest, the claim to it is not time-barred in the way a contract claim would be. This is a real argument that a Texas oil-and-gas attorney should evaluate, and it is not a research-file conclusion.
Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code § 16.004 (fraud), § 16.051 (residual four years), Tex. Prop. Code § 75.101 (three-year mineral-proceeds abandonment), § 74.501 and § 74.504 (Comptroller claims; hearing statement of findings is a public record)Statutory framework for the fraud claim, the residual limitations period, and the unclaimed-mineral-proceeds regime§ 74.504(b) means if the Comptroller decides any claim on this family’s money, the written statement of findings is a public record — obtainable. And § 16.065 provides that a written acknowledgment of a barred debt revives it.
Tex. Nat. Res. Code §§ 91.402, 91.404, 91.406Payment deadlines; a payee must give written notice of non-payment and the payor has 30 days to respond; and a successful payee recovers reasonable attorney’s fees and a minimum $200 awardThe procedural step, and it is cheap. § 91.404(b) means a certified-mail notice to the payor starts a 30-day clock, triggers the payor’s own records, and preserves fees and the minimum award. This is a letter, not a lawsuit.

The two inputs this section needed are now in hand. The payor is Truth Resources, L.P. and the operator is Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc., and the tract mapping is complete — see section 13I. The remaining unknown is the payment ledger itself, which is held by the payor and the operator and is obtainable on request under Tex. Nat. Res. Code §§ 91.404(b) and 91.501–91.505. That is now a named request to a named company rather than an open-ended search.

Why the limitations history may not be the fatal problem it appears. The claim has three separate components with three different accrual dates, and they are not interchangeable: the 1/96 reserved royalty (a property interest, accruing on each production payment, with the most recent ones plainly unbarred); the surface and residual mineral interest (a claim to recover real property, which § 16.051 expressly excepts from the residual four-year period); and any fraud claim (four years under § 16.004, subject to fraudulent concealment). Which component carries the value determines which limitations analysis governs — and the 2001 filings’ own overstatement is a reason the value-bearing component may not be what the family assumes.

Authorities verified against the published opinions and the Texas statutes; quotations and holdings are as stated in the cited opinions. These are research notes, not legal advice, and no limitation period, accrual date, or characterization of the 1931 reservation as property or contract has been adjudicated in this matter. Shell Oil Co. v. Ross, 356 S.W.3d 924 (Tex. 2011); Bright & Co. v. Holbein Family Mineral Trust, 995 S.W.2d 742 (Tex. App.—San Antonio 1999, pet. denied); Yowell v. Granite Operating Co., 620 S.W.3d 335 (Tex. 2020); Kerlin v. Sauceda, 263 S.W.3d 920 (Tex. 2008) (fraudulent concealment and laches do not extend limitations for royalty heirs of a 19th-century Spanish land grant where the claim could have been discovered with reasonable diligence — factually the closest analogue in the case law, and adverse); Harrison v. Bass Enterprises Production Co., 888 S.W.2d 532 (Tex. App.—Corpus Christi 1994, no writ); Wagner & Brown, Ltd. v. Horwood, 58 S.W.3d 732 (Tex. 2001); Jarvis v. Lovin, No. 12-17-00403-CV (Tex. App.—Tyler Oct. 10, 2018) (declining to extend § 16.065 beyond creditor-debtor settings); Tex. Civ. Prac. & Rem. Code §§ 16.004, 16.051, 16.065; Tex. Prop. Code §§ 74.501, 74.504, 75.101; Tex. Nat. Res. Code §§ 91.402, 91.404, 91.406.

13K. The Texas mineral accounts, the current title map, and the names that have never been searched

Added October 21, 2026 · Orange CAD mineral and surface accounts read in full · the family’s own ClaimItTexas results read against the recorded chain

This section closes the loop between three things that were previously separate: the four Texas mineral royalty accounts on the ancestral lease, the current tax-roll title map of the ancestral abstract, and the family’s own unclaimed-property search results. The result is a single chain that runs from a name on a Texas Comptroller record, through a lease on the Railroad Commission’s roll, to a name on the Orange County tax roll today — and it identifies precisely which searches were never run, and why the ones that were run could not have found them.

1. Lease 03-03243 (CORBELLO -A-): the complete account table, including the four family accounts

The Orange County Central Appraisal District carries the mineral accounts for the ancestral lease under their own account numbers, and each one states the lease, the wells, the abstract and the interest fraction in the legal description. Every mineral account reachable from the family’s surface accounts and from the operator’s accounts was pulled and read. Lease #360 CORBELLO -A- (RRC lease 03-03243), wells W#16-18, Abstract 10 William M. Dyson, is held as follows:

CAD accountOwner of recordFractionWhat it is
N318942KILMARNOCK OIL CO0.814814 WIThe operating interest
N318181KILMARNOCK OIL CO0.003750 RIThe operator’s own royalty slice
N319691CORBELLO, GEORGIA A0.010522 RIFamily-named royalty
N319690CORBELLO, JEFFREY A0.005261 RIFamily-named royalty
N322519ADAMS, THOMAS0.006845 RIFamily-named royalty
N322518ADAMS, CLAY B — LEVY PROCEEDS0.006845 RIFamily-named royalty, levy-marked
N318343PALERMO, KIMBERLEE CORBELLO0.005261 RIFamily-named royalty — see section 13L
Total royalty interest held under family names on this lease0.029473 + 0.005261= 2.9473%, plus 0.5261% if the Palermo account is live and separate

Four findings, each of which changes a document request. First: the four family-named accounts are confirmed to sit on the ancestral lease itself — every one of them reads “LEASE #360 CORBELLO -A- W#16-18 … ABST 10 WM DYSON SUR.” Until this pull, the briefing knew those names from the royalty roll but not their account numbers or their fractions; those are now fixed, and they are quotable in a demand letter. Second: Georgia A. Corbello’s interest is exactly twice Jeffrey A. Corbello’s (0.010522 ÷ 0.005261 = 2.0000), and the two Adams accounts carry identical fractions (0.006845 each) — the signature of a division order computed once from a single heirship schedule, not of separately bargained interests. Third: all four accounts, and the five surface accounts on tracts 393 and 394, carry the same mailing address in the CAD record — C/O KIM PALERMO, Lake Charles, Louisiana. The ADAMS, CORBELLO, McCALLISTER and STACY accounts are not four unrelated owners; they are one administered group, and Kim Palermo is the contact point through which the whole block of interests is administered. Fourth: the “LEVY PROCEEDS” suffix on N322518 is a garnishment marker on a family account, not a stranger’s name — the account is in the family block and the suffix records that a levy was served against that interest. A levy is a public fact that a payor would have disclosed to a claimant asking about that account, and it is a reason the check for that account may have been drawn to someone other than the family.

A caution about the CAD search method, because it changes how the searches must be run. The district’s public owner-name search is not exhaustive and silently omits accounts: a search for ADAMS returns nothing and a search for THOMAS returns nothing, although accounts owned by “ADAMS, LILLIAN CORBELLO” and “THOMAS, JEFFREY DAN & TIFFANY FELICE” plainly exist. The reliable method is the tract graph — each property record links to the other accounts that share the same underlying parcel — and that is how the tables in this section were built. The public CAD record also exposes only owner name, owner address, legal description and property location; it does not display appraised values, which still have to come from the district or the tax office.

2. The current title map of the ancestral abstract — the family is on the roll today, under married names

Pulling the tract graph from the family’s own accounts reveals something the briefing had not established: the family’s branches still hold title in the ancestral abstracts, under the married surnames that the unclaimed-property registries would report. These are the accounts, as they appear on the Orange County tax roll today:

Why this table is worth more than any dollar figure on the page. It puts the recorded heirs’ married surnames — FONTENOT, ROMERO, REEVES, LEBLEU, FELICE — on the current Orange County tax roll inside the ancestral abstracts, and it puts a single current holder of a 1/8 undivided interest in a 50-acre tract in the very abstract of the 1931 mineral deeds (Tract 341, held by Jeffrey Dan Thomas and Tiffany Felice, Orange, TX). It also shows the two branches married into each other: a Thomas (the Martha Lovett Thomas line) holds the tract with a Felice (the Martha Louise Gilley Felice line, daughter of Idellar Lovett Gilley). The search protocol in section 13I searches by birth surname; this table shows the family is titled by marriage surname. One further item: the CAD record for one account renders the care-of line with a typographic variant, C/O KIM PARLERMO — a second spelling to search alongside PALERMO.

One more thing about Ms. Palermo, established since this section was written: she is not only a titleholder and the care-of contact — she is a conveyancer into the family’s own land company. On 14 October 1997 KIMBERLY CORBELLO PALERMO conveyed her undivided interest in the 34.146-acre tract of the William Dyson League A-10 to C & E LAND COMPANY (OPR Vol. 1046, pp. 992–994, instrument 171159), and on the same day GEORGIA ALLEN CORBELLO conveyed hers (Vol. 1046, pp. 995–997, instrument 171160); Lillian Corbello Adams had done the same on 26 September 1997 (Vol. 1046, p. 229, instrument 170962). All three were women of this family, conveying into one company, and that company conveyed its oil, gas and other minerals interests to Cow Bayou Holdings, LLC in January 2021 — see section 13P. The Palermo accounts are therefore not a dead end and not a stranger’s holding: they are the family’s own mineral chain, documented in the county index.

And the tract numbers now identify the document to pull. Tracts 393 (4.725 acres) and 394 (8.5 acres) are the two parcels the family holds undivided percentages of, and the briefing has already identified an instrument in the family’s own file that describes exactly two tracts of exactly those acreages — the 1988 LeBleu donation, OPR Vol. 689, pp. 118–121, which the briefing lists but has never pulled. If that deed created tracts 393 and 394, it is the partition: the one document that would show who took what percentage of each tract, from whom, and on what date. It is the highest-value outstanding Orange County request, and it is now precisely framed. The 1961 partition deed (DR Vol. 286, p. 517), the 1959 decree (Vol. 235, p. 542) and the Exhibit A plat to the 1991 Enron easement (OPR Vol. 774, pp. 152–154) remain the test for whether Tract 341 is the 50-acre John Granger tract on which the 1931 deeds reserved the 1/96 royalty.

3. The family’s own ClaimItTexas results: one category, nine rows, and about $44,800

The family’s own search of the Texas Comptroller’s unclaimed-property registry (ClaimItTexas.gov, searched by hand because the site is bot-gated) returned on the order of 120 rows across five surnames. All but a handful are insurance refunds, dental credits, refunds and small balances — noise. Nine rows are oil-and-gas money, and they are the only rows in the entire result set that are provably about this industry:

Case no.Reported nameProperty type as reportedPayorAmountYear
35687845MARTHA LINDSEY THOMASMINERAL ROYALTYEOC CO$43,888.801991
