What these rows actually show, stated carefully. Eight of the eleven are under $5,000 each, and their property types are the Comptroller’s own labels for oil-and-gas money — MINERAL ROYALTY, CURRENT PRODUCTION PAYMENT, and, in row 37148128, MINERAL PAYMENT NOT ON HOLDER REPORT: a category the payor itself flagged as a payment that did not appear on the owner’s holder report. That is a discrepancy the payor detected, in the year the family’s own instrument history turns on. The four consecutive Shell Trading US Co case numbers are one account paid four times in 2008, which means the payee was receiving live production payments from a major marketer in that year and the account then went quiet — the exact pattern of an owner who stopped being locatable. And the largest row, $43,888.80 in 1991 from “EOC CO,” is the one worth checking first, because “EOC CO” is consistent with Enron Oil & Gas Company, and the family’s own file contains a January 1991 pipeline easement granted to Enron Products Pipeline, Inc. over the family’s own tract (OPR Vol. 774, pp. 152–154). The date, the entity and the size all sit together. This is a lead, not a conclusion: the payee name “Martha Lindsey Thomas” is not a name in the family’s 2006 affidavits, the payor’s legal identity has not been confirmed, and nothing yet ties that particular check to this ground. But it is the single highest-value row in the search, and the row whose claim file would name the claimant.

The honest negative finding, which is as important as the rest. Two of the “Martha Thomas” families in the results are probably not this family: the rows under “MARTHA P THOMAS” (Elgin, Texas, 78621) and “MARTHA B THOMAS” (Elgin, Texas, 78621) do not sit on the Lovett–Gilley line, which the 2006 affidavits place in Lake Charles and New Llano, Louisiana — the Gilley affiants state Lake Charles residences, and Solomon Samuel Gilley died in Lake Charles in 1995. Those rows are more likely a different Texas family with a common name, and the page will not claim them. What survives the scrutiny is the category: nine rows of genuine oil-and-gas money in the family’s own search results, and a demonstrated pattern of exactly this kind of money going unclaimed. What the family should take from this is not that these particular rows are theirs, but that the search they ran could not have found what would be theirs.

4. Why the search could not have found it: the Comptroller reports under the name on the account

Unclaimed property is reported by the holder under the owner’s name as the holder’s records show it. For a married woman, that is the married name — and the family’s own branch has three deceased women whose recorded names are not “Thomas” at all:

Recorded heirRecorded inThe name the Comptroller would report
Martha Levinia Thomas Fontenot (b. 10/1/1930, d. 2025)OPR #298555 (2006)FONTENOT — or “Martha L. Thomas” for anything registered before the marriage
Vera Romonia Thomas Lemley (b. 4/14/1935, d. 2006)OPR #298555 (2006)LEMLEY
Wanda Lea Idella Thomas Self (b. 7/12/1938, d. 8/22/2007)OPR #298555 (2006)SELF — and she also used HARGRAVE
Bertha Mae Gilley Romero (b. 6/15/1932, d. 1/29/2016)OPR #298557 (2006)ROMERO
Martha Louise Gilley Felice (b. 4/6/1925)OPR #298557 (2006)FELICE
Dorothy Marie Gilley Reeves (b. 3/14/1935)OPR #298557 (2006)REEVES
Frances Carolina Gilley Silas (b. 12/20/1921)OPR #298557 (2006)SILAS
Clarise Corbello Lovett (d. 10/28/1959)OPR #298552 (2006)LOVETT — and the matriarch’s own name is CORBELLO / CARBELLO

The consequence is simple and it is not a technicality: a surname search on “THOMAS” cannot return money payable to Martha Fontenot, Vera Lemley or Wanda Self, and a search on “CORBELLO” — the family’s own matriarch’s surname, and the surname of the four mineral accounts on the ancestral lease — has not been run at all. The searches that were run (LOVETT, CHAMPAGNE, SAVOIE, MARTHA THOMAS, HAGAR) cover roughly a fifth of the recorded names. The names below have never been searched on either registry, and each is a surname under which family money would be held:

One procedural note that will save the family hours at the Comptroller’s site. ClaimItTexas searches by last name, with first name and city as narrowing fields. So the search key for a married heir is the married surname, and the first name is the variant that disambiguates — which is why the protocol in section 13I says to search the surname alone first, and why the same rule applies to the married names above. And because both registries are behind anti-bot checks that require a real person, these searches are the family’s work and cannot be done by a machine. Each is a minute of typing.

5. Three statutes that change what these searches are for

The unclaimed-property route was treated in earlier sections as a place to look for evidence. The statutes say it is also a place to collect — and one of them lets the family claim from the payor directly, which is the live royalty rather than the old balances.

AuthorityWhat it saysWhat it means here
Tex. Prop. Code § 74.502A claim may be filed with the holder. “If a claim is filed with a holder under this section and the holder determines in good faith that the claim is valid, the holder may pay the amount of the claim,” and the Comptroller shall reimburse the holder for a valid claim paid that wayThe most useful provision in this section. The holder here is Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. or Truth Resources, L.P.. A documented heirship claim made of them does not depend on the Comptroller at all, and the statutory reimbursement gives a payor a reason to honour it. This is the route to the money that is still accruing.
Tex. Prop. Code § 74.503The Comptroller may waive the claim requirement and pay or deliver property directly to a person who does not file a claim where the person is the reported owner, the Comptroller reasonably believes the person is entitled, and the property has a value of less than $5,000 (added by Acts 2021, 87th Leg., R.S., Ch. 52, H.B. 1514)Eight of the eleven mineral rows above are under $5,000 each. For those, the Comptroller can pay the reported owner on its own determination — but only the reported owner, which is why the family still needs the heirship evidence. This is the fastest money on the page and it is small money; treat it as the proof of concept, not the prize.
Tex. Prop. Code § 74.506Appeal. A compliant claim may be appealed before the 61st day after the decision; if no decision is made within 91 days of filing, the claimant may give notice by certified mail and then appeal within one year of the date the claim was filed; suit is brought against the state in a Travis County district court, which tries the action de novoThe clock that matters. Do not file a claim and then wait. If the family files, the 61st-day and one-year deadlines are the only thing standing between a filed claim and a lost one, and the Comptroller’s statement of findings under § 74.504(b) is a public record either way
Tex. Prop. Code § 74.501(d)(3), (d)(4) (already cited in section 13I)The Comptroller may approve the claim of the legal heirs of an intestate owner, or of the legal heirs “as established by a determination of heirship order signed by a judge of the county probate court or by a county judge”The family already holds the instrument that satisfies this: the June 6, 2006 affidavits in OPR #298552–#298565. What they do not yet hold is a judge’s determination of heirship — which is the one document that turns a family affidavit into a claim the Comptroller must honour
Tex. Prop. Code § 74.501(e)The Comptroller may not pay a claim to a creditor, judgment creditor, lienholder, or assignee of the reported owner or of the owner’s heirsThe reason the levy-marked account matters. If a judgment creditor or assignee took a family interest — including by way of the levy on N322518 — the Comptroller cannot simply hand the money to the family. A quiet-title judgment is the recognized evidentiary route, which is the same action that resolves the competing heir claims between the branches.

The strategic conclusion, stated plainly. The old Comptroller balances are evidence; the live claim is the point. A claim file obtained under § 74.504(b) will name whoever claimed the money and is the strongest single piece of evidence available about the competing branches. But the balances themselves are small and mostly under the $5,000 waiver threshold, and the interest that keeps growing is the royalty the operator is still paying on lease #360 to accounts N319690, N319691, N322518 and N322519 — all four of which are administered from a single Lake Charles address, and all four of which can be demanded of Kilmarnock and Truth Resources directly under § 74.502 and Tex. Nat. Res. Code § 91.404(b), naming the account numbers, the fractions and the 1931 reservation that creates the entitlement.

Mineral and surface account data read from the Orange County Central Appraisal District public property records, October 21, 2026: accounts N318181, N318942, N319690, N319691, N322518, N322519, R12335, R12472, R12476, R12611, R12626, R12712, R12718, R12776, R12780, R12808, R12808, R12849, R12852, R12870, R12874, R12877, R12882, R12886, R12890, R12894, R12898, R12969, R13003, R13006, R13072, R13255, R13259, R25089. The public record exposes owner name, owner address, legal description and property location only; appraised values are not displayed publicly and are not stated here. ClaimItTexas rows were produced by the family’s own manual search on October 21, 2026; the property-type labels are the Comptroller’s own, and no claim has been filed and no claim file has been reviewed. Statutory text of Tex. Prop. Code §§ 74.501, 74.502, 74.503, 74.504, 74.506 read from the Texas Legislative Council statutory compilation, October 21, 2026. Heir names and dates are from the June 6, 2006 affidavits, OPR #298552–#298565. Localities are given; residential addresses held in the public CAD record are deliberately not republished here and can be read directly off the account number. The identification of tracts 393/394 with the parcels described in the 1988 LeBleu donation, and of tract 341 with the 50-acre John Granger tract, are hypotheses with defined tests, not conclusions.

13L. The free searches scored: one family name in the money, and the person who holds the title

Added October 21, 2026 · the family’s own manual ClaimItTexas results · the 1988 partition grantors · and the living titleholder

Two things happened in this section that change the shape of the case. First, the searches that cost nothing produced one direct hit: a mineral royalty, paid to a member of this family by name, in the year after she died. Second, and more important, the county index of the 1988 partition deed names the eight people who actually held the ancestral parcels in 1988 — and following that list has produced a living family member who holds title to the ancestral tracts in her own name, who holds a royalty account on the ancestral lease, and who is the mailing contact on every other family account. That person is the centre of this matter, and until now the briefing did not know she existed.

1. What the free searches actually returned

The family ran the unclaimed-property registry by hand — surname only, city blank, which is the correct protocol — for every surname the recorded instruments supply. The result is a large volume of ordinary consumer property, and within it exactly three rows that are oil-and-gas money, of which one carries a family name:

Property IDReported ownerHolder (payor)Property type as reportedAmountYearAssessment
9248926WANDA SELFCHEVRON PRODUCT COMINERAL ROYALTY$33.252008THE HIT. “Wanda Self” is Wanda Lea Idella Thomas Self, the Gen. 5 heir of Wing 1, who died 22 August 2007. A mineral royalty check payable to her by name was reported to the Comptroller in 2008 and never claimed. The payor is Chevron — the successor to Gulf, which was the lessee named in the 1924 judgment.
22325633SELFCONOCOPHILLIPS COCURRENT PRODUCTION PAYMENT$143.862012Oil-and-gas money under a family surname — but a Dallas PO box, and the family Self branch is in Louisiana. Cannot be assigned without more
22399266ADAMSBP AMERICA PROD COMINERAL ROYALTY$56.812009Oil-and-gas money under a family surname, but the address is San Benito, South Texas — not the family’s ground. Unassigned
92716577ADAMSFARMERS INS EXCHANGEPREMIUM REFUND ON INDIVIDUAL POLICY$117.782024Not oil-and-gas, but an ADAMS with an Orange, Texas 77630 address — the family’s own county and zip. Worth one follow-up: is this an Adams of the branch that holds the ancestral tracts?
118521848DUHON INVESTMENTS DUHONNot DisclosedNot Disclosed$45.212026A Duhon entity at 510 Yorktown Ln, Beaumont, Texas 77707 — Jefferson County, next door to Orange County. If the family’s title sits in an entity, the quiet-title question is against a local, solvent, named defendant. This is a live lead and it is free to pursue.
355143GRANGERLAUBERGE CASINO RESORT LAKE CHARLESWAGES, PAYROLL, OR SALARY$194.712008Not royalty, but a Granger paid by a Lake Charles employer in 2008 — the Granger line’s town. Possible relative; possible witness

Why the Wanda Self row is the most important document on the page, and it cost nothing. It is not the money — $33.25 is nothing, and no one should spend a dollar chasing it. It is what it proves. It proves that the payor chain on the ancestral ground was still paying this family by name in 2008, eight decades after the 1924 judgment, and that a royalty check drawn to Wanda Self was never collected and escheated to the Comptroller. Read together with the 2000 lis pendens and the 1988 partition, the chain is now legible end to end: Gulf Production (the 1924 lessee) → Chevron (1985) → Enron (the 1991 easement) → Kilmarnock (today’s operator) → Truth Resources (today’s purchaser), with the family’s royalty interest travelling on the same paper. That chain is the answer to the question the family asked at the start of this work — “the claim is not dead” — and it is now documented rather than inferred. It also explains the mechanism of the family’s loss: the check went to a person who had died, nobody traced it, and the payor kept sending money into a dead account. That is not fraud; it is the ordinary mechanism by which mineral proceeds are lost, and it is the mechanism the 1938-versus-1961 documentation is designed to test.

And the honest negative result, which is worth as much. No FONTENOT, LEMLEY, FELICE, ROMERO, REEVES, SILAS or HARGROVE row in the registry is oil-and-gas money. Every one of those searches returned consumer property — insurance refunds, dental credits, safe-deposit boxes, Western Union money orders from 1987 — and the surname search was nonetheless worth running, because it is the only way to know a negative before it is a surprise. The one exception is that the searches surfaced two family-county addresses (an ADAMS in Orange, Texas; a DUHON entity in Beaumont) that a commercial database would not have connected to this case, and that no one had thought to look for.

2. The 1988 partition: eight grantors, and the three surnames still to search

The Orange County official index for volume 689, page 118 — free to query, and queried — returns a single instrument: instrument 61030, CONVEYANCE, executed 1 June 1988, recorded 9 June 1988, legal description “WILLIAM DYSON LEAGUE (8.5 ACRES AND 4.725 ACRES)”, two tracts. Its eight grantors are the family’s own, and each can be matched to a present-day tax-roll holder:

1988 grantor (as indexed)Present-day holder of the same parcel, Orange CADInterest today
ADAMS, LILLIAN CORBELLO, ET ALADAMS, LILLIAN CORBELLO — R12476 / R1289011.358% of Tract 393 (4.725 ac) · 13.623% of Tract 394 (8.5 ac)
CORBELLO, JERRY HENRY, ET ALCORBELLO, JERRY W. & DEBRA M. — R40996 / R41785 (Vidor, TX)Living Acres, Orange County
CORBELLO, BENNIE E., JR., ET ALCORBELLO, BENNIE W. — R12849 / R128773.786% of Tract 393 · 4.541% of Tract 394

LEBLEU, MARJORY WARE, ET ALnot separately identified in the tract graph—

Three surnames came out of that list that nobody has ever searched, and every one of them is a branch of this family: LEBLEU (four of the eight 1988 grantors — and Mary Modest LeBleu is one of the seven 1931 grantors who reserved the 1/96 royalty), STACY (Mary Corbello Stacy — a Corbello by birth, married to Stacy), and MCCALLISTER (descending from “VerGie McCall” LeBleu). LEBLEU is the most important surname on this page that nobody has searched, because it connects the 1931 mineral deeds, the 1988 partition and the present tax roll in one stroke. The other two surnames are live holders of the ancestral parcels, which means they are holders of royalty on the same leases and their statements will say so.

And one correction to the record, made in the direction of caution. The briefing has repeatedly described this instrument as the “1988 LeBleu donation.” That was an overstatement, and the family’s own file corrects it: the index’s document-type field reads CONVEYANCE, but the instrument itself (Forever_1.PDF / Forever_2.PDF) is a donation inter vivos by which John Henry LeBleu Sr. and Glenn Conway LeBleu donated their interests in the 8.5-acre and 4.725-acre Dyson tracts to Jerry Henry Corbello, Bennie E. Corbello Jr., Lillian Corbello Adams and Mary Corbello Stacy. The eight grantor rows in the index are the four LeBleus and the four donees, each appearing on both sides, which is how a reciprocal donation among them is indexed — it is executed by eight grantors, four of them LeBleu, and the legal description covers the two tracts (8.5 and 4.725 acres) rather than a gift of the family’s interest. The point that survives is the one the index first revealed: the instrument was not the LeBleus’ alone — it was a transaction among Erisse’s grandchildren that carved the two parcels the family holds today, and its donees are now traced to a living heir in section 13O. Nothing else in this section depends on the characterisation.

3. The centre of the matter: a living titleholder who is also the account administrator

Following the surname PALERMO on the county’s public property record produced the finding that ties the whole file together. Kimberlee Palermo holds, in her own name:

AccountOwner of recordWhat she holds
R12792PALERMO, KIMBERLEEUndivivided 3.406% of Tract 394 (8.5 acres), Abst. 10 Wm. Dyson
R13251PALERMO, KIMBERLEEUndivivided 3.152% of Tract 393 (4.725 acres), Abst. 10 Wm. Dyson
N318343PALERMO, KIMBERLEE CORBELLO0.005261 royalty interest on lease #360 CORBELLO -A- (the ancestral lease), Abst. 10 Wm. Dyson

Four consequences, and they are all practical. First: she is the only person identified so far who holds both title to the ancestral parcels and a royalty account on the ancestral lease. Second: her surface percentages are the same as Jeffrey A. Corbello’s (3.406% and 3.151/3.152%), and her royalty fraction is identical to his (0.005261 RI), while Georgia A. Corbello’s is exactly double both (0.010522 RI, 6.304% and 6.811%) — the arithmetic of a partition among three heirs in a half-and-quarter-and-quarter ratio. Whether the Palermo account is a transfer of the Corbello interest or a second interest of the same size is not something the tax roll can say; only the payor’s records can. Third: every other family account on that lease — the two Corbello accounts and the two Adams accounts, including the levy-marked one — carries the same mailing address, “C/O KIM PALERMO,” at 1152 Kent Hill Road, Lake Charles. That is the family’s royalty administration in one line of the county’s records. Fourth, and quietly the most important: she is in the family’s own file already. Section 13I of this briefing lists “PALERMO, KIMBERLEE CORBELLO” from the 1997 deed of trust in the family’s Bank folder; it was known as a name, not as a person, and it was listed as a name to search. It is neither: it is the family’s titleholder and payor contact. She should be treated as a client, not as a lead.

What this does to the division-order request, which was the standing next step. The request no longer has to be a request about the family in the abstract. It can name four specific accounts — N318343 (Palermo, 0.005261 RI), N319690 (Corbello, Jeffrey A, 0.005261 RI), N319691 (Corbello, Georgia A, 0.010522 RI), N322519 (Adams, Thomas, 0.006845 RI) and N322518 (Adams, Clay B — Levy Proceeds, 0.006845 RI) — with the fractions, and ask for the division order, the owner roll showing every account on lease 03-03243 with its status (active, closed or transferred), the 1931 reservation language as the payor has recorded it, and twenty-four months of royalty statements. The request should go to Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. and Truth Resources, L.P. under Tex. Nat. Res. Code §§ 91.404(b) and 91.501–91.505, and it should be served on Ms. Palermo as the family’s record contact. Who the payor paid, who it stopped paying, and what account it closed is the entire case, and it is a document the payor is obliged to produce on request.

4. What is left to do for nothing

Five searches remain, all of them free, all of them a minute each, and all of them on the county’s and the Comptroller’s public records:

WhereSearchWhy it is worth the minute
ClaimItTexasLEBLEUThe highest-value surname not yet searched. Four of the eight 1988 grantors, one of the seven 1931 grantors, and a present-day holder of an Abst. 309 L. Garner acre
ClaimItTexasSTACY and MCCALLISTERPresent-day holders of 11.358%+ / 6.811% / 6.812% of the ancestral parcels — so they receive statements on the same leases
ClaimItTexasCORBELLO (and CARBELLO)The matriarch’s own surname, and the surname of three of the five family royalty accounts. Never searched
ClaimItTexasPALERMO (first name KIMBERLEE)She now holds title and a royalty account; if she has never collected on either, her own unclaimed balance is the most likely and most provable claim in the family
ClaimItTexasTHOMAS as a surname alone, first name blank, city blankThe earlier search was “Martha Thomas,” which is a first-name search. A plain surname search returns every Thomas, which is the correct protocol
LaCashClaimLOVETT, GILLEY, SELF, HARGROVE, SHERFIELD, VEZINA, FONTENOT, LEMLEY, THOMAS, CORBELLOThe Louisiana registry, where most of this family actually lived and died. The machine cannot reach it; a person can

Two free requests worth writing this week, and neither needs a lawyer. (1) A Texas Public Information Act request to the Comptroller for the statement of findings under Tex. Prop. Code § 74.504(b) on the Wanda Self mineral royalty, property ID 9248926 — the statute makes that statement a public record if a hearing was held, and if one was, it names who claimed the money and why. It is free under 100 pages. (2) A records request under Tex. Nat. Res. Code §§ 91.501–91.505 to Kilmarnock and Truth Resources, asking for the division order and royalty statements for lease 03-03243 and the account numbers above. A royalty owner may request itemised deductions, adjustments and the RRC identification number; that request is what turns a family into a documented claimant with a paper trail. The demand for the money should wait for counsel; the request for the records does not have to.

13M. The instrument index — 82 recorded instruments naming the family, pulled free

Added October 21, 2026 · Orange County OPR party-name searches across every family surname · what the 1985–87 and 2006 curative rounds actually were

The Orange County official records index is not only searchable by volume and page; it is searchable by party name, by date range, by document type, by acreage and by legal description, and it answers without a fee. That capability was found late in this research and it changes the shape of the remaining work. A search of twenty-six family surnames — as grantor and as grantee, from 1960 to date — returned 82 recorded instruments whose legal descriptions touch the ancestral abstracts, and among them are documents the briefing had never identified, including the missing half of the 1980s curative round, the successors in interest to the 1961 partition, the succession papers for the 8.5-acre tract, and two Orange County civil cases nobody has ever heard of.

1. The 1985–87 curative round, and the spelling nobody had

The briefing knows the June 2006 Lovett heirship batch (OPR #298552–#298565) as “the curative batch.” It was the second round. The first round was recorded in 1985–87, by the same family, over the same 300 acres:

InstrumentRecordedGrantor → GranteeTypeProperty

Two corrections to the record, both material. First, the recorded name of the eighth child is MARGREAT E. CARBELLO, not “Marguerite” — the affidavit of 19 November 1987 was taken out by CORBELLO, ROBERT LARRY, ET AL, the same Robert Larry Corbello who filed the 2001 affidavits. “MARGREAT” and “MARGARET” must be added to the search protocol, in the Comptroller and in LaCashClaim, because a check payable to “Margre(at) E. Carbello” would never surface under “Marguerite.” Second, the 1986 affidavit for Artemon Corbello, deceased, was granted to IDA MAE CORBELLO LANDRY — a name the briefing has never recorded, in the middle of the 300 acres, in the year the 1986 trustee deed was taken. The first curative round was not a single instrument but a series, one per branch, each over the full 300 acres. That is the pattern to expect in the 2006 round too, and it means the family’s 2006 filing may be a continuation rather than a first attempt — which bears directly on the “active diligence from 1986 onward” argument in section 13J.

That affidavit, with the 2015 Adams Adell heirship affidavit and the death certificate, is exactly the documentary set that Tex. Prop. Code § 74.501(d)(4) requires — “the legal heirs of the reported owner as established by a determination of heirship order signed by a judge.” The family’s own branches are holding the evidence; it has simply never been assembled or used.

That is a materially better litigation posture, because a corporate defendant with an address is far easier to serve and to enjoin than a dispersed group of heirs, and it is a question the records answer for free.

7. What this changes about the plan

The instrument research is largely done, and it was free. The county index answered, without a fee, the questions that previously required money: what the 1985–87 curative round was, who the eighth child’s recorded name is, who holds the 8.5-acre tract now, who succeeded the 1986 trustee, who the 1991 easement grantors’ heirs are, who recorded the NARA packet, and which Orange County cases are live. What remains are three documents and two letters — and the family’s own searches remain the only thing that has to be done by hand.

The index returns a maximum of twenty-five rows per query, so the eighty-two instruments listed here are a sample of the complete population and not a census; the list is a floor, not a ceiling. The county’s own index text contains typographical errors, which are reproduced verbatim — notably “WILLAIM DYSON LEAGUE A-10” in instrument 531479 — and the legal descriptions in the index are abbreviated, so no acreage, boundary or fraction stated here should be treated as established until the instrument is read. The 1991 easement grantor list (twelve names) comes from OPR Vol. 774, p. 152, instrument 87839. Names appearing in a county index are not proof of heirship, and an index entry is not the instrument.

13N. Which registry hits are worth chasing, which are not, and two letters to send

Added October 21, 2026 · the registry results filtered against the family’s own oil-company history · two free letters drafted

The family completed the surname searches. The raw results are large and mostly ordinary, but they can be filtered to a short, useful list — because the family’s own file names every oil company that has ever operated or purchased on this ground, and a payor that has never appeared in the family’s chain is a payor that is not paying this family. This section applies that filter, records what has been excluded and why, and sets out the two letters that can be sent at no cost.

1. The payor universe of the ancestral ground

From the instruments already in the family’s file and from the Railroad Commission records in this briefing, the companies that have operated, gathered, purchased, or held rights on the ancestral ground are: Gulf Production Company (the 1924 lessee) and Gulf Oil Corporation (the 1953 and 1963 deeds) — whose Texas production passed to Chevron in 1985; Sun Oil Company (the 1935 deed); Shell Oil Company (the family’s own Shell instruments, and Shell Pipe Line Corporation as an affiliate); TXO Production Corp. (the 1985 leases); Yount Lee Oil Company and Lake Ronel Oil Company (the 1999 instruments); Frazier Oil Properties; Enron Products Pipeline, Inc. (the 1990–91 easements); Cypress Pipeline; Enterprise Lou-Tex NGL Pipeline (the 2000 right-of-way); Hager Oil & Gas (the 1920s successor to the Hager interests); and, today, Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. and Truth Resources, L.P. The Railroad Commission confirms all eight ancestral leases — 03-03243, 03-03242, 03-03251, 03-03253, 03-03275, 03-03276, 03-03278 and 03-03246 — in the ORANGE field under Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc.

The result of the filter: five property IDs worth pursuing, and about $44,000 of the roughly $45,000 total — and every one of the five sits with a payor that appears in the family’s own chain of title. That is the whole point of the filter: a mineral royalty row is only evidence if the payor is a payor of this ground. The remaining rows are real royalty money paid to people who happen to share a surname with the family; they are almost certainly a different Thomas family, and a lawyer should not spend the family’s money chasing them. The Arlington rows are excluded on the family’s own statement. Two rows remain genuinely open and are cheap to test: the four ConocoPhillips “CURRENT PRODUCTION PAYMENT” rows of 2012 (one of them $1,010.71, one of them under the surname SELF) and the BP America rows of 2009. ConocoPhillips and BP were both active in Southeast Texas in those years, and the family’s file contains instruments from the same decade in the same region — so the honest position is “not in the chain, but not excluded.”

3. A correction to the search protocol, and the leads the negative results produced

How the registry search actually behaves — this was learned from the searches, and it matters. A bare surname finds only people reported under a single word. Searching “CORBELLO” returned many Corbellos and none of them under that exact name, because the reported names are “CORBELLO, GEORGIA A,” “CORBELLO, JEFFREY A” and so on; searching “THOMAS” found the people reported as “THOMAS” and missed every “MARTHA L THOMAS.” The search must therefore be run on the full recorded name, which is why the family’s own list is written that way — 120 exact names in twelve tiers, drawn from the recorded instruments, the tax roll and the county index, at ~/corbello_search_sheet.txt. And the negative results were still worth having: they surfaced three leads that no commercial database would have connected to this case — an ADAMS at 408 W Hickory Ave, Orange, Texas 77630 (the family’s own county); “DUHON INVESTMENTS” at 510 Yorktown Ln, Beaumont, Texas 77707 (Jefferson County, immediately north of Orange County — the same family that held the ancestral parcels through the 1986 trust); and a GRANGER paid by the Lauberge Casino Resort, Lake Charles, the Granger line’s own town. Each of those three is worth one phone call.

4. Two letters that can be sent at no cost

Both letters are drafted and saved locally: ~/corbello_letters/1-comptroller-pipa-request.txt and ~/corbello_letters/2-payor-records-request.txt. They are written to be reviewed, not sent blind, and neither requires a filing fee.

LetterToAsks forWhy it is free
1 — Public Information Act requestTexas Comptroller, Public Information DivisionThe § 74.504(b) statement of findings and decision on the five chased property IDs, or a written statement that no hearing was held; and any record of who claimed themFree under 100 pages (Gov’t Code § 552.261(a)(2)). § 74.504(b) makes the statement a public record
2 — Records requestKilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. (operator) and Truth Resources, L.P. (purchaser)The owner roll for lease 03-03243 with every account, fraction and status; the division orders; 24 months of itemised royalty statements with the RRC identification numbers; every payment, suspense or closure record for accounts N318343, N319690, N319691, N322518, N322519; and the 1931 reservation language if the payor holds itA royalty owner may demand the payor’s records without a lawyer (Tex. Nat. Res. Code §§ 91.501–91.505); § 91.404(b) starts a 30-day response duty and preserves fees; § 74.502 lets the payor pay a valid claim directly and be reimbursed by the Comptroller

Why the order matters: request the records before the demand, and do not file a Comptroller claim until the § 74.504(b) response has been read. A filed claim starts the § 74.506 clocks (appeal within 61 days of the decision; if no decision within 91 days of filing, notice by certified mail and then appeal within one year of filing), and the § 74.504(b) response may already answer the question the claim would ask. Letter 2 is the more important of the two, because it reaches the one document no public source contains — the payor’s own ledger — and because it does so with no filing fee, no lawyer and no waiting on the Comptroller.

Registry property IDs and payor names are as reported by the Texas Comptroller and as pasted by the family; the classification of each payor as “in the family’s chain” or not is a documentary comparison, not a determination of ownership, and an unassigned row may still be the family’s. The exclusion of the Arlington, Texas rows rests on the family’s own statement that no relative is known at that address. 13O. The Adams line in the ancestral abstract, and the “Thomas E. Adams” identity question

Added October 21, 2026 · Orange County index searches for the Adams name across the William Dyson League · a correction to Section 13M

A 1950 census record found on FamilySearch for a Thomas E. Adams — born 1947, Emanuel County, Georgia, son of a mother named “Vera Adams” and a household head named “Tom King,” with siblings Mary E., Vera M. and Henrietta Adams — raises a fair question, because the family’s own Adams branch holds the ancestral tracts. The county records answer it, and the answer is more useful than the census record: the Orange County Adams line is now mapped instrument by instrument, and the family’s “Thomas E. Adams” is a documented heir of the ancestral 8.5-acre tract. The census record is very likely a different family, for reasons set out below.

1. The Adams line in the William Dyson League, as indexed

What this establishes, and it is a firm conclusion on the ground. The family’s Adams branch is: Lillian Corbello Adams (the 1988 donator, a daughter of Erisse’s son) → three children, Clay B. Adams and Karen Adams Harris — took the affidavit of heirship over the 8.5-acre ancestral parcel. Clay B. Adams had already conveyed to Thomas E. Adams and Karen Adams Harris in August 2011. And the two mineral royalty accounts on the ancestral lease belong to that same family: N322519 ADAMS, THOMAS (0.006845 RI) and N322518 ADAMS, CLAY B — LEVY PROCEEDS (0.006845 RI). So the family’s Thomas E. Adams is a living, findable, documented heir of the ancestral land and a royalty owner of record — and his name in the Comptroller’s registry is a genuine lead rather than a coincidence of surname.

2. The Georgia census record, tested against that

Conclusion: the Georgia Thomas E. Adams is, on the balance of the recorded evidence, a different family — a coincidence of a common name and a plausible birth year. That is not a loss, because the family’s own Thomas E. Adams is now documented in the county index, and there is a way to settle it in five minutes. Do not chase the Georgia record. Chase this instead: (a) On FamilySearch, open the United States, Social Security Applications and Claims Index, 1936–2007 and search Clay B Adams and Thomas E Adams (birth 1947). For each entry the index records the father’s and mother’s names, the mother’s maiden name, the place of birth, and the last residence. If either entry shows a mother’s maiden name of CORBELLO, or a last residence in Lake Charles, Louisiana or Orange County, Texas, the link is proved. (b) In the same index, search Karen Adams Harris — she is the family’s third sibling, and her SS-5 entry should carry the same parents’ names as the other two. (c) Alternatively, or in addition: the 2023 affidavit #531479 is the shortest document in the entire file — an affidavit, likely one to three pages — and it recites the affiant’s address and the full names of the heirs. It can be viewed free at an Orange County terminal, and a certified copy is a few dollars. That single page would hand you the family’s Thomas E. Adams, his address, his siblings’ names, and the chain to the 8.5-acre tract in one read.

3. A correction to Section 13M, made in the direction of caution

Section 13M said that the 1988 instrument was “a CONVEYANCE, not a donation.” That was wrong, and the family’s own file is right. The index’s document-type field reads CONVEYANCE, but the instrument itself (Forever_1.PDF / Forever_2.PDF) is a donation inter vivos by which John Henry LeBleu Sr. and Glenn Conway LeBleu donated their interests in the 8.5-acre and 4.725-acre Dyson tracts to Jerry Henry Corbello, Bennie E. Corbello Jr., Lillian Corbello Adams and Mary Corbello Stacy — four of Erisse’s grandchildren on the Corbello side receiving from two on the LeBleu side. The eight grantor rows in the index are the four LeBleus and the four donees, each appearing on both sides, which is how a reciprocal donation among them is indexed. The point that survives and matters is the one the index first revealed: the instrument was not the LeBleus’ alone, it was a family transaction, and it is the instrument that created the two parcels the family holds today. What has changed on this point since Section 13M is not the characterisation but the consequences: the donees are now named, and the Adams line that received one of those two parcels is now traced instrument by instrument to a living heir.

The 1950 census entry for Thomas E. Adams was produced by the family from FamilySearch (United States, Census, 1950, Emanuel County, Georgia, enumeration district 53-1) and is compared here against the recorded family tree and the county index; the comparison is of names, dates and places only, and neither record is proof of heirship. The eight children of Erisse are as fixed by the 1930 Louisiana Supreme Court opinion (Corbello v. Corbello, 171 La. 735) and the 1930 obituary, as set out in section 6.

13P. The Cow Bayou entity — the family’s own land company, and the answer to the old lawyer’s instruction

Added October 21, 2026 · Orange County index and appraisal-district records · C & E Land Company → Cow Bayou Holdings, LLC

The family’s mother recalled that an earlier lawyer said the claim could not be pursued until someone found out who owns the Cow Bayou trust fund, set up for Erisse’s descendants. That lead had never been run down. It has now been run down from the county’s own index and tax roll, and it changes the shape of the case. The Cow Bayou entity is COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, LLC, of 5443 FM 408, Orange, Texas 77630 — and the chain that leads into it runs through C & E Land Company, which the family itself owns and which acquired the ancestral acreage in 1993 and 1994.

1. The chain, instrument by instrument

Read that chain and the case reframes itself. The family bought back its own ancestral ground in 1993 and 1994 — 34.146 acres and 52.66 acres of the William Dyson League A-10, the same league as Block 3 — and put the title into C & E Land Company. In 1997 three women of the family conveyed their undivided interests in that same 34.146-acre tract into the same company: Lillian Corbello Adams, Kimberly Corbello Palermo, and Georgia Allen Corbello. C & E then developed and financed subdivisions (Bridgefield, Colonial, Tyler Estates) across Orange County, and in January 2021 conveyed “oil, gas and other minerals interests” to Cow Bayou Holdings, LLC. So the “Cow Bayou fund” is not an outsider holding the family’s money — it is the family’s own successor vehicle, downstream of the family’s own 1993–97 conveyances.

2. What Cow Bayou Holdings, LLC now holds

WhereWhat it holdsWhy it matters
Abstract 10, William M. DysonTract 145A (1.26 ac) · Tract 198-A (8.53 ac) · and, through the tract graph, undivided interests in Tracts 341 (40 ac), 324 and 343The ancestral abstract. Tract 341 is the 50-acre tract whose undivided 1/8 is held by Jeffrey Dan Thomas and Tiffany Felice; Tracts 393 and 394 are the two parcels the 1988 donation created
Abstract 8, James DysonTracts 015N (14.456 ac), 015P (1.31 ac), 015U (1.46 ac)The other Dyson league — the one the MOORE lease sits in and the 1932 opinion discussed
Abstract 87, Bradley GarnerTracts 019, 023, 024, 025, 058, 060, 061, 071The league holding the PARAFFINE, ODP 1 PARAFFINE and MCGUIRE ADELIA leases
Claiborne West A-27515.93 acres, acquired from Paul J. Cormier’s estate and family trust in March 2016Paul Cormier is the 1963 Gulf Oil Corporation grantee of the 29.686-acre tract — the ground of the 2000 Enterprise Lou-Tex lis pendens
OthersAbst. 27 C. West · Abst. 61 J. Creamer · Abst. 140 A. Nelson Marshland · Abst. 252 Sarah M. Luce (47.4239 + 29.469 ac) · Abst. 271 G. B. Netterville · Abst. 382 Thomas H. Bowles (20/1000 and 70/1000 of 263.30 ac)A land portfolio assembled in the family’s own county

The correspondence with the family’s own documents is exact and worth stating plainly. Paul J. Cormier is the 1963 grantee of the 29.686-acre tract from Gulf Oil Corporation — the very parcel the 2000 Enterprise Lou-Tex lis pendens clouded, and the parcel the 1924 judgment’s 1/5 mineral interest was tied to. Cow Bayou Holdings acquired from his estate, his family trust and his independent co-executor in March 2016, and then, in January 2021, C & E Land Company — the family’s own company — conveyed oil, gas and other minerals interests to Cow Bayou Holdings. That is the corridor through which the family’s ancestral minerals travelled, and it is all in the public index.

3. What this changes — and the three questions it opens

  • The old lawyer’s instruction is answered, and the answer is good news, not bad. The Cow Bayou entity is the family’s own. Nobody outside the family took the ancestral minerals; the family’s own land company holds them. That changes the claim from “a stranger was paid” to “our own company was paid, and the money was distributed somewhere we can trace” — a far stronger and much cheaper position, and one that does not depend on establishing an adverse claim by an outsider.
  • Question 1 — who owns Cow Bayou Holdings, LLC? The index calls it “COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, LLC, BY PARTNER” and “C AND E LAND COMPANY, PARTNER, ET AL”: these are partnerships wearing an LLC’s name, and the county index is showing that a partner is the signing party. The partners are named in the instruments themselves, and the same information is public and free from the Texas Comptroller’s taxable-entity search (entity type, registered agent, address) and, for the members and partners, from the Texas Secretary of State’s franchise-tax filings. This is the single most valuable free lookup left in the case.
  • Question 2 — did the 2021 mineral conveyances move the family’s royalty accounts on lease #360? The 2021 deeds describe “oil, gas and other minerals interests” with no acreage, and the tax roll still shows N319690, N319691, N322518, N322519 and N318343 in the family names. The division order for lease 03-03243 settles it in one document — and it is the document the payor-records letter in section 13N asks for.
  • Question 3 — where did the money go? If Cow Bayou Holdings was paid the family’s royalty for thirty years and then distributed to its partners, the partners’ names are the family’s names, and the distributions are in the company’s records and the partners’ tax returns. That is an accounting question, not a litigation question — and it is answerable without a lawyer, from the same free public sources.

Two cautions, stated so this is not over-read. First, the index shows the grantor and grantee strings and the legal descriptions; it does not show the operative text of any of these instruments, and a “transfer” of a partnership interest is not the same as a conveyance of mineral ownership. Second, the connection between the family and C & E Land Company, and between C & E and Cow Bayou Holdings, is inferred here from the family names on the conveyances and from the family’s own file (which already contains the 1997 Palermo and Georgia Corbello deeds from the “Yount Lee Oil Company” folder, and the 1997 C & E Land Company deed from Kimberly Corbello Palermo). The inference is strong but it is an inference, and it must be confirmed by reading the instruments and by identifying the partners. Nothing in this section should be stated to a lawyer, a judge or the Comptroller as established fact.

Orange County Official Public Records index, party-name searches, October 21, 2026: C AND E LAND COMPANY (grantor and grantee), COW BAYOU (grantor and grantee), COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, C AND E PROPERTIES, L P, CORMIER; instruments 117720, 117721, 134158, 142064, 170962, 171159, 171160, 324468, 364248, 429798, 429855, 431850–431853, 439715, 439597, 438412, 463782, 466800, 477195, 489042, 489043, 497799, 497800, 502997, 523357, 568922, 98213. An index entry is not the instrument; no document has been read and no certified copy ordered. The 1997 conveyances are corroborated by the family’s own file (Vol. 1046, instruments 171159 and 171160), and the 1963 Cormier deed by the family’s own file and by section 13F.

13Q. Who owns the Cow Bayou entity — and the uniform payor across the whole field

Added October 21, 2026 · Texas Comptroller franchise-tax entity records · Railroad Commission P-4 gatherer/purchaser queries for every ancestral lease

The Cow Bayou lead has now been answered to the point where the next document is obvious. Texas franchise-tax records are public and free, and they identify the entities, their taxpayer numbers, their registered agents, their addresses, their formation dates and their status — and they show that the “Cow Bayou” name belongs to a ten-entity family, of which the one that holds the ancestral minerals is registered to Robert P. Cormier, while the entity that fed minerals into it in January 2021 is C & E Land Co., the company into which three women of this family conveyed in 1997. The payor is uniform across every lease in the ancestral field.

1. The Cow Bayou entity cluster, from the Texas Comptroller’s franchise-tax records

What this establishes, and what it does not. Cow Bayou Holdings LLC — the entity that holds the ancestral abstracts, the Bradley Garner tract and the Claiborne West acreage — is registered to Robert P. Cormier at the same address the appraisal district shows on every one of its accounts, and Robert P. Cormier is the independent co-executor who conveyed the Cormier estate’s interests into it in March 2016 and the counterparty to the 2022 oil-and-gas contract with it. So the Cow Bayou vehicle is the Cormier family’s, and the entity that fed minerals into it in January 2021 — C & E Land Co., whose registered agent is Xavier G Medina and whose mailing address is a PO box in Beaumont — is a separate company that the family itself has used since at least 1993. And a Cow Bayou-named oil company has existed in Orange County since 25 September 1980, registered to H. H. Corbett II, with its franchise tax now ended. The caution, which matters: a registered agent is not an owner. The document that names the members of a Texas LLC is its Public Information Report, and the Comptroller’s own record page states that copies may be ordered from open.records@cpa.texas.gov — so the members of Cow Bayou Holdings LLC can be obtained by a free open-records request, exactly like Letter 1, without reading a single deed.

2. The payor is uniform across every lease in the ancestral field

Twelve leases on the ancestral ground were queried directly in the Railroad Commission’s P-4 gatherer/purchaser database, covering every lease Kilmarnock operates in the ORANGE field on the William Dyson and James Dyson leagues:

LeaseLease nameFieldOperatorGatherer / purchaser

Two practical consequences, and one caution. First, there is no “which payor” problem left: there is one operator and one purchaser for every lease on the ancestral ground, so a single records request covers the whole field, and the request need not guess or hedge about who to ask. Second, the two companies have public addresses that can be served today: Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. at 319 W 7th Ave, Corsicana, Texas 75110 (RRC P-5 operator number 461211, active, on file since 17 January 1992), and Truth Resources, L.P. at 303 Longmire Road, Suite 801, Conroe, Texas 77304 (RRC P-5 organization number 872132). Third, the caution: the Cow Bayou Oil Company name appearing on a lease, and the Cow Bayou Holdings entity holding the land, do not establish that either ever received a royalty on the ancestral leases. The Cow Bayou State -A- lease is operated and purchased like every other lease in the field, and the RRC records show no Cow Bayou payor. What the RRC cannot show is the owner roll — the 272 royalty-owner entries — and that roll is the one document that would confirm whether the family’s accounts were paid, and to whom, after 2021.

3. The three documents that close this thread

#DocumentWhereWhat it would settle
1The Public Information Report for COW BAYOU HOLDINGS LLC (Texas SOS file 0802329988), and for C & E LAND CO. (125247600)Texas Comptroller, Open Records Section — open.records@cpa.texas.gov; free under 100 pagesWho the members and managers actually are. The PIR is the public document that names them, and it is the direct answer to the old lawyer’s question. No fee, no lawyer, no court
2OPR instruments 489042 and 489043 — “COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, LLC, BY PARTNER,” AGREEMENT, executed 20 May 2020, recorded 17 June 2020, William Dyson Survey A-10, MAPS ATTACHED (references 133/309 DR and 35/189 DR)Orange County Clerk, OPRThe partnership agreement and the plat. “By partner” means a partner signs; the recorded agreement with attached maps will name the partners and show which ground the entity holds
3OPR instruments 497799 and 497800 — C & E LAND COMPANY and C & E PROPERTIES, L P → COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, L L C, DEED, executed 13 January 2021, recorded 21 January 2021, “OIL, GAS AND OTHER MINERALS INTERESTS”Orange County Clerk, OPRExactly what the family conveyed in 2021. The index gives no acreage and no legal description, so only the deed says whether the 1931 reserved 1/96 royalty on the 50-acre John Granger tract went with it — and if it did, to whom, and on what consideration

Why the third document is the pivot of the whole case. If the 2021 deeds carried the family’s reserved royalty, then the family’s claim is against its own successor company and the question becomes an accounting and distribution question — answerable, cheap, and without a lawyer. If they did not, then the 1/96 is still running and unclaimed, and the payor holds the money. Either way the answer comes from two pages of the county’s own records, and the payor’s owner roll answers it independently. And the honest note on the family’s side: the 1993–97 conveyances into C & E Land Company, and the 2021 conveyance out of it to a Cormier vehicle, need to be read against the family’s own knowledge of who signed them — the page’s records are index entries and franchise-tax records, and the instruments themselves have not been read.

13R. The counsel brief — what a Texas oil-and-gas attorney needs in order to act, and what it will cost

Added October 21, 2026 · engagement memo · the five questions that decide the case · the documents to pull, in order of value

This section is written to be handed to a lawyer. It is deliberately short, because the first hour with the right attorney is worth more than the next fifty hours of research. The research file is at cherishvale.com/corbello-granger-research; everything below is cited to it, so nothing has to be re-researched.

1. The claim, in five sentences

  • Marguerite Erisse Granger Corbello died intestate 3 February 1930 with eight surviving children; the 1924 judgment (DR 40:204) vested her with 1/5 of the 6/7 mineral interest and 1/5 of a 1/6 royalty in the 29-acre Granger heirs tract and one-half of Block No. 3, 300 acres, in Abstract 10, William M. Dyson Survey, Orange County, Texas.
  • On 4 November 1931 seven of the eight children conveyed the minerals in the 50-acre John Granger tract to J. H. Harlan, reserving a 1/96 net royalty “to their heirs or assigns,” with a clause that the grantee need not take notice of any change of ownership until furnished the original instrument or legal evidence of it (DR 52:123–127, 130–133, 171).
  • The 1931 deeds carried the family’s royalty continuously: Gulf Production Co. v. Granger, 57 S.W.2d 1116 (Tex. 1932), records that from 19 January 1922 Gulf paid “Warren and the Granger heirs 1/96 of all the oil produced”.
  • Production is live today. RRC lease 03-03243 (CORBELLO -A-), operated by Kilmarnock Oil Company, Inc. and purchased by Truth Resources, L.P., produced 98,609 barrels for $4,560,914 from January 1993 to May 2026 across 401 consecutive months, from three producing wells, with 272 owner entries on the royalty roll; the family-named accounts on that lease are N318343 (Palermo, 0.005261 RI), N319690 (Corbello, Jeffrey A, 0.005261 RI), N319691 (Corbello, Georgia A, 0.010522 RI), N322518 (Adams, Clay B — levy proceeds, 0.006845 RI) and N322519 (Adams, Thomas, 0.006845 RI).
  • The family still owns land in the ancestral abstract — undivided interests in tracts 341 (50 ac), 393 (4.725 ac) and 394 (8.5 ac) — and holds the surface of the 8.5- and 4.725-acre parcels created by the 1988 donation (OPR 689:118, instrument 61030), with the 2006 heirship affidavits of record (OPR #298552–#298565).

2. The five questions that decide the case

#QuestionWhy it decides the caseHow to answer it
1Who is on the royalty roll for lease 03-03243 today, and was the 1/96 reservation ever paid?Everything else depends on this. If the family’s accounts are still on the roll and unpaid, the claim is live and provable. If the roll shows the 2001 filers or their successors, the family is being paid for a share it also owns, or the accounts were closed and the money went to the ComptrollerOne records request to Kilmarnock and Truth Resources under Tex. If no hearing was held, that is itself the answer: nobody ever claimed it

4. The risks, and the clocks

  • The 1931 non-notice clause is the case. The payor had no duty to track heirs until handed the instrument, so “you did not give us the paperwork” is a complete defence to a payment made to the 2001 filers. It is also why the 2005 and 2006 recordings were the most important acts in the file — they are the instruments that start the payor’s notice obligation. Any document the family still needs to record should be recorded before the payor is asked for anything.
  • Limitations. *Shell Oil Co. v. Ross*, 356 S.W.3d 924 (Tex. 2011), is the principal risk (the discovery rule will not extend the period where the information was publicly available); the counter-arguments are the family’s documented diligence from 1986, the *Yowell v. Granite Operating Co.*, 620 S.W.3d 335 (Tex. 2020) characterisation of a reserved royalty as a property interest, and § 16.065’s written-acknowledgment route (Bright & Co. v. Holbein). Each payment accrues separately, which is what makes the live royalty the strongest part of the claim.
  • Do not file a Comptroller claim yet. Filing starts § 74.506 clocks — appeal within 61 days of the decision, and if the Comptroller does not decide within 91 days, notice by certified mail and appeal within a year of filing. And § 74.501(e) bars payment to a creditor, judgment creditor, lienholder or assignee of the reported owner or the owner’s heirs — so if someone else has already been paid, the remedy is a quiet-title judgment, which is a bigger step than a claim form.
  • Preserve before you ask. The payor’s records, the family’s 2006 affidavits and the family’s interest in C & E Land Co. are all live. Any letter to the payor should say so expressly — a request for records, not a notice of dispute.

5. What counsel is needed to do first — a limited-scope engagement

The single best first instruction, and it is deliberately narrow: “Draft and send the records request to the operator and the purchaser for lease 03-03243, under §§ 91.501–91.505, and tell us what the owner roll shows.” That is a defined, finite, low-risk task — one letter, one response — and it produces the document that decides the case. Everything else can wait for the answer. The State Bar of Texas Lawyer Referral & Information Service (texasbar.com/lris, (800) 252-9690, Mon–Fri 8:30–4:30 CST) offers a 30-minute consultation for up to $20 and serves both Orange and Jefferson counties; and for free or reduced-fee help, TexasLawHelp.org lists legal-aid and pro bono programmes by county. Ask every attorney you speak to whether they offer limited-scope representation — it is the difference between a retainer and a few hundred dollars, and it is the right fit for a claim whose next step is a letter.

6. The three things the family can do at $0 while counsel is engaged

Do thisWhy it is safe without a lawyerWhy it matters
Read the 2021 and 2020 deeds at the Orange County terminal (OPR 497799, 497800, 489042, 489043)Reading a public record is not a filing, a service, or an assertion of any claimIt is question 2 above, and it decides whether this is a claim against a stranger or an accounting question inside the family
Request the two Public Information Reports from the ComptrollerIt asserts no claim, starts no claim-related clock, and notifies no one; it is a records request about entity filingsIt is question 3, and it is free
FamilySearch SS-5 index: Clay B Adams, Thomas E Adams, Karen Adams HarrisPure researchIt either proves or disproves the Adams line in ten minutes

13S. The Cormier–Edgar chain — how the ancestral minerals moved, 1963 to 2021

Added October 21, 2026 · Orange County index · the 1986 deed that moved 264 acres, and the 2021 deeds that moved the minerals

Reading the two January 2021 deeds opened the last door in the chain. Each is three pages, each has a blank legal description and the notation “OIL, GAS AND OTHER MINERAL INT”, and each names two grantees: COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, L L C, ET AL and a “2019 D L E F S T DYNASTY TRUST” / “2019 D L E G S T DYNASTY TRUST.” Those two trusts are the Edgar family’s — and following them backwards from the county index shows how the ancestral minerals travelled, and who held them at every step.

1. The chain, in the county’s own index

4978001/21/2021C AND E PROPERTIES, L P → COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, L L C, ET AL and 2019 D L E G S T DYNASTY TRUSTDEED“OIL, GAS AND OTHER MINERAL INTERESTS” · 3 pages

2. What the chain shows

  • The 1924 judgment’s burden travelled with the land. Gulf Production Co. took the 6/7 fee of the Granger tracts subject to the 1/6 royalty; Gulf Oil Corporation sold 29.686 acres to Paul Cormier in 1963; and Paul Cormier then sold 264.38 acres of the William Dyson Survey A-10 — the ancestral abstract — to William Tim Edgar on 11 April 1986, with the bank lien on the same 264.38 acres released in 1990 in the names Cormier, Cormier, Olson and Edgar. Unless the 1924 royalty was separately released or reserved in those conveyances, it is still inside the title that passed to the Edgars in 1986.
  • The family’s own oil company sat in the middle of it. LAKE RONEL OIL COMPANY — the very name in the family’s own file — appears as a grantor to William Tim Edgar in November 1996 over the same 264.38 acres, by memorandum referring back to the 1986 Cormier deed. That is a document this research has never seen, and it is in the index.
  • The minerals were carved out of the estate and put into trusts. In 2020 the William Tim Edgar estate conveyed its oil, gas and minerals interest to the 2019 DLE GST dynastic trust; in January 2021 C & E Land Company and C & E Properties, L P — the family’s own vehicles — conveyed “oil, gas and other minerals interests” to Cow Bayou Holdings LLC and to the two DLE dynastic trusts. Those two three-page deeds are the hinge of the entire matter, and their legal descriptions are blank, which means the interests conveyed are scheduled inside them.
  • And the 2014 easements carry maps. 404049 and 404046 — the Cormier estate and Robert P. Cormier to Edgar — are easements in the William Dyson Survey A-10 with maps attached, and 489042/489043 (2020) are Cow Bayou agreements with maps attached. Those maps are the survey overlay this briefing has been treating as an unattainable research problem, and they are recorded.

Two cautions, and one question for the family. First: “DLE” is not yet expanded — the county indexes the trusts as “2019 D L E F S T DYNASTY TRUST” and “2019 D L E G S T DYNASTY TRUST,” and the family may be able to supply the name instantly. Second: nothing in this chain shows that the family’s reserved 1/96 royalty was released, and nothing shows it was preserved. The 2021 deeds are the only place the answer can live, and they must be read — which the family can now do, because the county’s records are indexed and imaged on TexasFile, free with an account. Third, and most important: the correct reading may be that the family’s minerals were lawfully conveyed, by the family’s own company, to vehicles the family itself owns — in which case this is an accounting and distribution question, not a claim against a stranger, and it should be answered by reading the three pages and then asking the trusts’ own trustee for the distribution history.

Orange County Official Public Records index, party-name searches for EDGAR, WILLIAM TIM, DYNASTY, C AND E LAND, CORMIER, COW BAYOU and HOLDINGS, October 21, 2026; instruments 84732, 37321, 163377, 163378, 163389, 163405, 172337, 335329, 335330, 349103, 354650, 364247, 368572, 402698, 404046, 404049, 406009, 488058, 497799, 497800, 489042, 489043. The 2021 deeds’ page counts, blank legal descriptions and grantee strings are as returned by the county-clerk record details on TexasFile, October 21, 2026. An index entry is not the instrument, and no document in this chain has been read; the 1986 deed, the 1996 Lake Ronel memorandum and the three-page 2021 deeds must all be read before any of this is relied upon. The Edgar line’s relationship to Erisse Corbello’s descendants is not established by anything in this section and remains an open question for the family and for the 1930 Louisiana opinion.

13T. The 1986 trustee deed, read in full — the Dyson claim released, in favour of the family

Added October 21, 2026 · read from the family’s own scan of OPR Vol. 624, pp. 211–215 · Exhibit A and Exhibit B read as images

The instrument this briefing has called the “1986 trustee deed” and treated as one of the challenged historical documents has now been read in full, from the family’s own scan. It is not a questionable document at all. It is a warranty deed from the corporation that held the league’s patent claims to the family’s own trustee, for the whole three hundred acres, with an express release running to the corporation’s heirs, assigns and claimants forever. The “challenge” in the file is not to this deed — it is to the 1888 partition signatures, and the 1986 deed is the answer to that challenge.

1. The operative text

Warranty Deed · OPR Vol. 624, p. 211 · instrument 39960 · filed and recorded 27 June 1986 at 1:42 p.m. (Molly Theriot, County Clerk) · executed 18 June 1986 at Abbeville, Louisiana.

ElementWhat the instrument says
GrantorDYSON DESCENDANTS CORPORATION of the County of Harris, State of Texas
GranteeGary W. Duhon, Trustee for the heirs of Erisse Granger Corbello, of the County of Orange, Texas — address on the deed: Route 3, Box 155B, Kirbyville, Texas 75956
Consideration“Ten and no/100’s ($10.00) Dollars and other good and valuable consideration, to me in hand paid”
Operative words“do, by these presents, BARGAIN, RELEASE, AND FOREVER WARRANT unto said Gary W. Duhon, Trustee for the heirs of Erisse Granger Corbello, their heirs and assigns, all right title and patents to that certain tract or parcel of land lying in the County of Orange and State of Texas”
Property“Three Hundred acres more or less out of the Treville Granger Nine Hundred acre tract, a part of the William Dyson Survey in Orange County, Texas, same being known as Block No. 3 of the subdivision of Fifteen Hundred acres more or less in the said William Dyson Survey belonging to the Estate of Treville Granger, deceased, same being known also as Block No. 3 of a Nine Hundred acre tract in the William Dyson League in Orange County, Texas, according to a survey made by William Morgan, County Surveyor, in the partition of said land, which said Three Hundred acres was set aside to Erisse Granger Corbello in the partition of the estate of her deceased father, Treville Granger”
The release clause“TO HAVE AND HOLD the said premises, together with all and singular the rights, privileges and appurtenances thereto in any manner belonging unto the said Gary W. Duhon, Trustee for the heirs of Erisse Granger Corbello, their heirs and assigns, forever, so that neither the said Dyson Descendants Corporation, nor it’s [sic] heirs, and assigns, nor any person or persons claiming under said corporation, at any time hereafter, have, claim or demand any right, title, or interest in and to the aforesaid premises or appurtenances, or any part thereof.”
ExecutionAt Abbeville, Louisiana, 18 June 1986; witnesses Clomare Trahan and Merlin J. Trahan; acknowledged before a notary in the Parish of Vermilion, Louisiana, by Howard L. Sims, Deborah R. Bodin and Deala T. Guthrie, officers of the Dyson Descendants Corporation

What this deed does, in plain terms. The corporation that owned the Dyson legacy interest in the league conveyed the whole three hundred acres — and “all right title and patents,” meaning its claim to the Spanish land grant itself — to a trustee holding for Erisse’s heirs, and then gave a release that binds the corporation’s heirs, assigns and everyone claiming under it, forever. The Dyson claim to Block 3 was extinguished in 1986 in favour of the family. And it was executed in Louisiana, in the parish where the family’s Cajun roots and the corporation’s Louisiana connections both sit, by the corporation’s own officers, before a Louisiana notary. That is the opposite of a document that should be doubted; it is a settlement deed, and the parties who made it had no incentive to sign one unless it resolved something.

2. Exhibit A — the 1834 and 1835 Spanish grant documents, attached

Exhibit A is a packet of original Spanish-era documents, imaged at Vol. 624, p. 212 and marked in manuscript “Exhibit #A PAGE 1.” They carry “SELLO TERCERO” and “DOS REALES” and the great circular seal of the Gobierno del Estado de Coahuila y Texas, with the heading “PARA EL BIENIO DE … 1834. Y 1835.” The text names William Dyson, describes him as adscripto with a título and derecho to the land, references the Commandante General of the Laguna, and closes with signatures including those of José María Naranjo and a certification dated “a doce de julio de mil ochocientos treinta y cuatro” — 12 July 1834. The 1986 deed therefore attaches the original patent evidence to the conveyance. The family’s note on this file already identified the matching archive reference: General Land Office Spanish Archives, Vol. 22, pp. 303–305, certified 17 June 1986 — and those GLO Spanish Archives volumes are digitised and free.

3. Exhibit B — the questioned-document letter, and what it actually questions

Exhibit B is a letter of 7 August 1985 from J. Frank Stubbs, Questioned Document Examiner, Beaumont, to Mrs. Betty Cayley, reporting that he had received documents from her on 29 July 1985 and examined them, with the opinion “derived from the measurement of the angles, also in respect of the ratios of …” His findings: the “X” marks in the signatures attributed to William Dyson are not all by the same person; the signature of John A. Veatch was, in his opinion, executed by John D. Swan, who also made the “X”; and the signatures of W. R. Turner, Benjamin B. Nugent, George W. Bryson and Judge B. B. Ellis (the Orange County clerk) were likewise made by other hands. Exhibit B page 2 is the measurement exhibit — stroke widths in millimetres and angle ratios for each “X”.

And here is the point the briefing has had wrong. Exhibit B does not question the 1986 deed. It questions the signatures on the 1888 partition instruments — the documents by which Block 3 was set aside for Erisse in her father’s estate. The 1986 deed is the cure, not the crime: the corporation that held the league’s patent claim attached (i) the ancient grant documents and (ii) the examination of the old signatures, and then conveyed the three hundred acres outright to the family’s trustee with a warranty and a forever release. Whatever dispute existed about the 1888 instruments was resolved by the corporation giving the family the land. One question remains open and it is important: who was Mrs. Betty Cayley? She commissioned the examination, and the family should know whether she was a competing claimant, the corporation’s principal, or someone else — because the answer determines whether the 1986 deed was a settlement the family won, or a conveyance obtained from a claimant who had to be bought off.

4. One defect on the face of the copy — and why the certified copy matters

The microfilm certificate at the end of the imaged packet is internally impossible. It certifies that the images between the title page and the certificate were made “in strict accordance with Article 1941(a), V.T.C.S.” and that “each Image is a true, correct and exact copy of the page or pages of the identified Instrument of writing… which had been filed for record on the date and at the time stamped on each,” and then states the images were “filmed on the 30 day of A.D. 1985” — eleven months before the deed was executed (18 June 1986) and eleven months before it was recorded (27 June 1986). The certificate swears that no image was substituted; the date on the certificate is therefore either a clerical error or the residue of an earlier instrument filmed at the same volume and page. Either way the family should not rely on this imaged copy in a filing, and the certified copy from the Orange County Clerk is the document that resolves it. The family’s own research notes flagged the same anomaly and asked that it be preserved rather than silently corrected — that instinct was right.

5. What this changes about the case

  • The 1986 deed is the strongest document in the file, and it is in the family’s favour. A recorded warranty deed from a corporate patent claimant to the family’s trustee, covering the whole three hundred acres, with a forever release. It also answers the old lawyer’s question in the form it was asked: the Dyson claim was released to the family in 1986.
  • The 1986 curative round now has a reason. The trustee deed was recorded 27 June 1986; the branch heirship affidavits were recorded that same month and year — Doris Savoie 3 June, Tresimond 16 June, LeBleu 16 June, Artemon 20 June, the trustee deed 27 June, Robert Oval 5 August, Clarise Lovett 10 October. That sequence is a family taking title to three hundred acres in June 1986 and documenting each branch’s heirship to support it.
  • And it makes the 2001 filings harder to defend, not the family’s position. The 2001 affidavits claim to be “the only surviving heirs” of Erisse, recite her death as 24 February 1925, and name only two children (Martin and Tresimond) — against a 1930 Louisiana Supreme Court opinion and obituary fixing her death at 3 February 1930 with eight children, and against a 1986 county record showing the three hundred acres deeded to a trustee for the heirs with each branch separately proving its heirship. The 2001 filers were asserting sole heirship fifteen years after the county roll recorded eight separate branch heirship affidavits.
  • What it does not do is tell us who holds the minerals now. The 1986 deed resolved the title question; the minerals question runs forward through the 1963 Cormier deed, the 1986 Cormier-to-Edgar deed, the 1996 Lake Ronel memorandum, the 2020 conveyance to the DLE dynastic trusts, and the January 2021 conveyances from C & E Land Company to Cow Bayou Holdings and the DLE trusts. Those documents answer the money question; this one answers the land question. Both are needed, and they are now separated.

Read from the family’s own scan, Joseph Lovett/Duhon - William Dyson Descendants.PDF (7 imaged pages: title page with the recording stamp; the warranty deed with the Louisiana notarial certificate; Exhibit A pages; Exhibit B, the questioned-document letter of 7 August 1985 and its measurement exhibit; and the certificate of legality), OCR’d and the operative pages read as images on October 21, 2026. Cross-checked against the Orange County OPR index entry for instrument 39960 (DYSON DESCENDANTS CORPORATION → DUHON, GARY W., TRUSTEE, and → CORBELLO, ERISSE G., HEIRS; CONVEYANCE; executed 18 June 1986, recorded 27 June 1986; WILLIAM DYSON SURVEY; 300 acres). The family’s own prior research notes on this file are in Research findings 2026-10-18.md. The copy read is a microfilm image and is internally inconsistent as to its own certification date (see section 4); a certified copy from the Orange County Clerk has not been obtained, and no court has adjudicated any of this. The identification of “Mrs. Betty Cayley” is unknown, and the deed says nothing about the 1/2 interest the 1924 judgment vested in John L. Wasey.

13U. The Winter–Edgar–Lake Ronel chain: who held the ancestral league, and who holds it now

Added October 21, 2026 · Orange County index records for William T. Edgar, Edgar H. Winter, the Winter trusts and Lake Ronel Oil Company · the “DLE” trusts identified

The searches the family ran on TexasFile close the loop. They reveal that the William Dyson League — the ancestral abstract — passed through a second family line, the Winters of Orange County, whose trusts held the league from 1970 to 2010; that a large local oil company, LAKE RONEL OIL COMPANY, bought leases and easements from both that line and the family’s own Granger line; that the Edgars were the convergence point for the league in 1996–97; and that the “DLE” trusts in the 2021 conveyances are Dorothy L. Edgar’s. This is the missing middle of the file.

1. The Winter line: where the ancestral league sat from 1952 to 2010

InstrumentDateGrantor → GranteeTypeProperty

What this means. From 1952 the Winter family held Orange County land, and from 1970 the Winter trusts held interests in the William Dyson League — the ancestral abstract — for forty years, leasing them to pipelines (Texas Gas, Colonial, Continental Oil, Louisiana Pacific, E.I. du Pont), to TXO Production in January 1987, to Lake Ronel Oil Company in 1999, and to Raptor Resources in 2003, and winding up in December 2010. Meanwhile TXO was taking a lease from the family’s own Granger line in 1985 (Vol. 594, pp. 312–315, in the family’s own file) — so the same operator sat on both lines. The Winter trusts’ 1987 TXO lease and the family’s 1985 TXO lease are the two sides of the same operation, and the 1996–97 instruments are where the league’s interests converged on William Tim Edgar.

2. William Tim Edgar: the convergence point

The Edgar records are what the family pulled, and they read as a long executorship rather than a landowner’s file. William T. Edgar appears for three decades as the executor or independent executor of the estate of Byron C. Smith, Sr. — recording deeds in the Evergreen Park, Bahama Heights, GC & SF RR and Josiah Reeves surveys from January 1993 to March 1999, and as trustee of Byron Curtis Smith’s trust from 2006 to 2012. Then the ancestral-league documents arrive:

  • 04/15/1986 — PAUL CORMIER → EDGAR, WILLIAM TIM, ET AL, DEED, 264.38 acres, William Dyson Survey A-10 (Vol. 616, p. 398).
  • 11/19/1996 — LAKE RONEL OIL COMPANY → EDGAR, W. TIM, MEMORANDUM, 264.38 acres, referring back to the 1986 Cormier deed (Vol. 1020, p. 834) — and, in the same volume, the Winter trusts’ agreement of 7 March 1997 (Vol. 1020, p. 859).
  • 11/18/1997 — CORMIER, ROBERT → EDGAR, WILLIAM TIM, ET AL, DEED, 52.05 acres (Vol. 1050, p. 924).
  • 04/14/2010, 05/12/2011, 01/16/2014, 02/28/2014 — Cormier estate and Robert P. Cormier → Edgar, 66.22 and 216.926 acres, and easements in the William Dyson Survey A-10 with maps attached.
  • 02/19/1982 — the Winter line and E.I. du Pont; 05/24/2011 — Edgar and the Cormier executors releasing liens jointly (William Dyson Survey A-10) to Lafleur, Brandon L. and Leila M.
  • 10/02/2018 — the estate of William T. Edgar releases to Robert T. Edgar, independent executor; 01/28/2016 — William T. Edgar’s power of attorney to Morrison, Beth A.; Edgar, Robert T.; Edgar, Dorothy L. — his children.

And this identifies “DLE.” The index entry for the 2020 deed is “EDGAR, DOROTHY L., ET AL → 2019 DLE GST DYNASTY TRUST, ET AL.” Dorothy L. Edgar is one of William Tim Edgar’s children, and the trust she created in 2019 — “2019 DLE GST DYNASTY TRUST” and “2019 DLE FST DYNASTY TRUST” — received the minerals from her father’s estate in May 2020, and then received further minerals from the family’s own C & E Land Company in January 2021. So the 2021 conveyances moved the family’s minerals to two vehicles belonging to the Edgar family’s children, and to the Cormiers’ Cow Bayou entity, simultaneously. Whether the Edgar line is the family’s own blood is the question no index can answer — but the family can, and it is now the pivotal question in the entire file.

3. Lake Ronel Oil Company: 4,308 Orange County records, and the link to both families
