Three things fall out of the Lake Ronel file that matter more than the rest. First: the 2018 deed to DUHON, CHERYL and DUHON, RICHARD. Gary W. Duhon was the 1986 trustee for the family’s heirs; his children now buy land from Lake Ronel. Second: the twelve powers of attorney of 18 August 2016 name the owners of Lake Ronel Oil Company — Younger, James David; Wright, Larry Kyle; Mazza, James J.; Patty, Cynthia M.; Graul, Patrick M.; Lake, Peyton M.; Blomdahl, William S.; Griggs, Fred J.; H. K. Harry; Lake, Peter M.; Miramar Services / Miramar Energy; Trajectory Investments. One 29-page assignment (to ORANGE L R E P, LLC) and a 23-page assignment (to GULF COAST WORKING PARTNERS, LLC) carry the whole portfolio with “SEVERAL REFS GIVEN,” which is the shape of a portfolio assembled from many parcels — including the ancestral league. Third: BP AMERICA PRODUCTION COMPANY appears twice as a payor/assignee from Lake Ronel — and “BP AMERICA PROD CO / MINERAL ROYALTY” is one of the payors in the family’s own ClaimItTexas results.

4. The question the index cannot answer

Everything above is public record and everything above is circumstantial about who is blood. But three facts narrow it to almost nothing: (1) the Winter line came into Orange County in 1952 from George E. Holland, and its trusts held the ancestral league for forty years; (2) H. J. Lutcher Stark — the son of Henry J. Lutcher of the 1889 First National Bank of Orange, and of the Lutcher line that the 1924 judgment itself involves — received Lake Ronel’s interests in 2014 through the Stark foundations, alongside agents named Brown, Lutcher Luis and Winter, Bruce L.; and (3) Lake Ronel bought from both the Winter line (1999) and the family’s own Granger line (1998–99 — the memorandum in the family’s own Bank folder, Vol. 1132, pp. 716–718), and in 2021 received minerals from the family’s own C & E Land Company. So: is the Winter–Edgar–Lake Ronel–Stark line the family’s own blood, or the Orange County families that bought and leased from them? And is Lake Ronel a company the family owns, or a company the family sold to? The family can answer both in a minute, and the answer decides whether this file is a claim against strangers or an accounting of the family’s own holdings.

Orange County official records index entries for William T. Edgar (14 results), Edgar H. Winter (25), EDGAR H WINTER TRUST (16), LAKE RONEL OIL COMPANY (4,308 across 87 pages), retrieved from the county-clerk record details on TexasFile, October 21, 2026; combined with the Orange County OPR index searches run October 21, 2026 for EDGAR, CORMIER, DYNASTY, COW BAYOU, C AND E LAND, C AND E PROPERTIES and HOLDINGS. The family’s own file corroborates the Lake Ronel and TXO instruments: Bank/Lake Ronel Oil Company*.PDF and Joseph Lovett/TXO Corp - Granger.PDF. No instrument in this section has been read; all of it is index data, and none of it establishes who is related to whom. Whether the Winter, Edgar, Lake Ronel, Stark, Duhon, Cormier and Granger lines are one family or several has not been determined, and the 1930 Louisiana opinion and the 1930 obituary have not been re-examined for these surnames.

13V. C & E Land Company is the family’s own — and in 2021 it sold minerals to third parties

Added October 21, 2026 · the C & E Land Company chain in the Orange County index · a correction to the family’s own sibling list

The index makes C & E Land Company’s identity plain: between 1993 and 1997 it was assembled by this family, out of this family’s own interests in the William Dyson League, and it was the family’s land company. It then developed and financed subdivisions across Orange County, and in January 2021 it conveyed oil, gas and minerals to the Cormiers’ Cow Bayou entity and to the Edgar family’s dynastic trusts. That is the fact that has to be looked at first, and it is why the three pages of those two deeds now outrank everything else in the file.

1. How the family’s company was built

InstrumentDateGrantor → GranteeProperty
117720 · Vol. 867, p. 6156/30/1993BRIDGE CITY ORANGEFIELD COMMUNITY → C AND E LAND COMPANY34.146 acres · William Dyson League A-10
117721 · Vol. 867, p. 6186/30/1993BRIDGE CITY ORANGEFIELD COMMUNITY → C AND E LAND COMPANY52.66 acres · William Dyson League A-10
135335 · Vol. 927, pp. 767–76911/02/1994PATRICIA ANNE CORBELLO PEACOCK → C & Ethe 34.146-acre interests
134158 · Vol. 924, p. 1389/28/1994BAKER, JANICE MCCANN → C AND E LAND COMPANY52.66 acres · William Dyson League A-10
142064 · Vol. 949, p. 8255/11/19957 UP TRUST, ET AL → C AND E LAND COMPANYBradley Garner Survey A-87
170962 · Vol. 1046, p. 22910/07/1997ADAMS, LILLIAN CORBELLO → C AND E LAND COMPANY34.146 acres · William Dyson League A-10
171159 · Vol. 1046, pp. 992–99410/14/1997PALERMO, KIMBERLY CORBELLO → C & E Land Co.34.146 ac within a 57-acre tract, Dyson A-10
171160 · Vol. 1046, pp. 995–99710/14/1997CORBELLO, GEORGIA ALLEN → C & E Land Co.the 34.146-acre tract, Dyson A-10

497799 · 4978001/21/2021C AND E LAND COMPANY; C AND E PROPERTIES, L P → COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, L L C, ET AL and 2019 DLE FST / DLE GST DYNASTY TRUST“OIL, GAS AND OTHER MINERAL INTERESTS” · 3 pages each

Read the middle of that table and the shape of the case becomes plain. Four members of the family — Patricia Anne Corbello Peacock, Lillian Corbello Adams, Kimberly Corbello Palermo and Georgia Allen Corbello — put their undivided interests in the 34.146-acre tract of the William Dyson League into C & E Land Company, and the company bought two more parcels of the same league (86.8 acres) from outsiders in 1993 and 1994. So by 1997 the family’s land company held the family’s own ancestral-league land. And in January 2021 that company conveyed oil, gas and mineral interests to two vehicles belonging to a different Orange County family line — the Cormiers’ Cow Bayou Holdings, and the dynastic trusts of the Edgars’ children. Two readings are possible and only the three pages of the deeds can choose between them: either the conveyances moved only C & E’s own purchased and consolidated minerals, leaving the 1931 reserved 1/96 royalty untouched and still running; or they carried the family’s inherited royalty with them. Nothing in this briefing can tell which, and a lawyer should not be asked to advise until it is known.

2. The third question the 2021 deeds now raise

If C & E Land Company was still the family’s company in January 2021, then the family’s company sold minerals in 2021, and counsel must know that before any demand is made on Kilmarnock or Truth Resources — because a payor told “these interests were conveyed in 2021” will answer very differently from one told nothing. And if it was no longer the family’s company, the family’s claim is now a claim against the company it sold to, and the question of when the family’s interest in C & E was sold becomes the first question in the file, not a footnote. The index offers two handles for the family. First, the 2008 deed is made by “C AND E LAND COMPANY, PARTNER, ET AL” — a partnership signing in 2008, which means the company’s own governing instrument names partners, and the family may know who they were. Second, the county’s franchise-tax record (Section 13Q) shows C & E LAND CO. with a registered agent of Xavier G Medina and a mailing address at a PO box in Beaumont, Texas, with its franchise tax ended — a company whose franchise tax has ended and whose agent is a Beaumont lawyer, in the county where the family’s claims have been fought. Both point to the same question: when did the family stop owning C & E Land Company?

3. A correction to the family’s own list, and a new name for the trust line

Two things in the family’s own research notes need correcting or confirming. First, Erisse and Charles Corbello had ten children, not eight: Mary Modeste (LeBleu), Tresimon, Artemon, Angeline (Champagne), Josephine (Duhon), Clarise (Lovett), Mary Dorise (Savoie), Amelia (1886–1900), Charles Jr. (1888–1913) and Martin — of whom eight survived her in 1930, which is why the one-eighth computation holds; the two who died as infants are the reason the file’s list reads “eight.” Second, the Duhon line is bigger than the file shows, and it is the Dyson line: the index has 47244 (1987) DYSON, WILLIAM, DECEASED → DUHON, WILLIAM BILL, ET AL, HEIRSHIP over the William Dyson League A-10; 52538 (1987) → DUHON, BILL, PATENT over the William Dyson Survey A-10; 253349 (2003) DUHON, BILL → DYSON, WILLIAM, ET AL, LEASE; and the family’s own file holds “2003-06-12_OPR-1364-p816_253349_Bill-Duhon-Legal-Notice.pdf.” So the family’s 1986 trustee (Gary W. Duhon) was himself in the Dyson line, and in 2018 that line bought land from Lake Ronel (Cheryl and Richard Duhon, David Odom Survey A-141). The trustee’s family is not a stranger to this land, and it is a live buyer in it.

Orange County Official Public Records index, October 21, 2026: instruments 117720, 117721, 134158, 135335, 142064, 170962, 171159, 171160, 318089, 319636, 324468, 364248, 401410, 429855, 439596–439598, 497799, 497800, 47244, 52538, 253349, 463564, 463565, 488058, 489042, 489043. The 1994 Peacock deed, the C & E records and the Duhon line are from the family’s own prior research notes (Research findings 2026-10-18.md, Records requests and overnight handoff 2026-10-18.md, Open-web research 2026-10-18.md) and the family’s scanned files in Documents/Family Documents/. Entity facts (C & E Land Co., registered agent Xavier G Medina, franchise tax ended) from the Texas Comptroller franchise-tax records, October 21, 2026. No instrument in this section has been read except the 1986 trustee deed (section 13T); the three-page 2021 deeds have not been read, and the identity of the interests they conveyed is unknown. Whether C & E Land Company was the family’s company in 2021, and what it conveyed, are open questions.

13W. Who holds the family’s company now, and who “Betty Cayley” was

Added October 21, 2026 · the 1997 Palermo deed read from the family’s own scan · Texas franchise-tax entity records · the Orange County index searched for Cayley

Two questions from the family were put to the record this round — who owns C & E Land Company now, and who Betty Cayley was — and both now have answers from public records rather than inference. Neither answer is comfortable, and both are exactly what a lawyer needs.

1. The 1997 deed, read from the family’s own file

Warranty Deed · OPR Vol. 1046, p. 992 · instrument 171159 · executed 2 October 1997, recorded 14 October 1997, 9:26 a.m. (Karen Jo Vance, County Clerk).

ElementWhat the deed says
GrantorKIMBERLY CORBELLO PALERMO
GranteeC & E LAND CO., P.O. Box 226, Orangefield, Texas 77639
Consideration“Ten Dollars ($10.00) and other good and valuable consideration”
Property“All of my undivided interest in 34.16 acres” — the parcel called to contain 57 acres in the William Dyson League, Abstract No. 10, Orange County, Texas, more particularly described by metes and bounds
Chain recitedSix deeds from C. C. Hubert to Yount-Lee Oil Company: 22 May 1928 (Vol. 46, p. 441); 24 July 1928 (Vol. 46, p. 302); 28 January 1929 (Vol. 47, p. 206); 5 February 1929 (Vol. 47, p. 245); 11 February 1929 (Vol. 47, p. 273); 23 February 1929 (Vol. 47, p. 315)

What it shows. The 34.146-acre parcel the family consolidated was not the 1931 parcel. The 1931 Harlan mineral deeds cover the 50-acre John Granger tract (deed of 13 June 1913, DR Vol. 14, p. 241). This parcel is a different one — the “57 acres” of the William Dyson League, whose title comes down through C. C. Hubert and Yount-Lee Oil Company in 1928–29. So there are two ancestral parcels in play, not one, and the family’s own 1997 conveyances moved the second of them. Which parcel the 1931 reserved 1/96 attaches to is now the first question in the file, not the third. And note what the deed says and does not say: it conveys “all of my undivided interest,” for ten dollars, with no reservation of any royalty, no reference to the 1931 reservation, and no exception — so if the 57-acre tract carries any part of the reserved royalty, that interest went into C & E in October 1997.

2. Who owns the C & E group now — verified from the state’s own entity records

EntityTexas taxpayer no. · SOS fileRegistered agentAddressStatus
C & E PROPERTIES, L.P.32036113556 · 014252010W. TIM EDGAR440 Eastex Freeway, Beaumont, TX 77703FRANCHISE TAX ENDED
C & E LAND CO.30116092120 · 125247600XAVIER G MEDINA1006 Pickfair, Austin, TX 78750 · mail PO Box 5134, Beaumont, TX 77726FRANCHISE TAX ENDED
CEO INVESTMENTS, INC.17605506819 · 146401200DAVID OLSON25 W Roundbunch Rd, Bridge City, TX 77611ACTIVE

This confirms the family’s lead, and it is the pivot of the whole matter. C & E Properties, L.P. — the entity that was a co-grantor of the January 2021 mineral conveyances — is registered to W. Tim Edgar at 440 Eastex Freeway, Beaumont. C & E Land Co. is run through a Beaumont lawyer whose franchise tax has ended, and the group’s active vehicle, CEO Investments, Inc., is run by David Olson at 25 W Roundbunch Road, Bridge City — the same street as Bridge City State Bank, the bank that took liens on the family’s ancestral ground and released them to C & E. And the 1990 release of the bank’s lien on the 264.38 acres was made to “CORMIER, CORMIER, OLSON AND EDGAR.” So the family’s land company is now in the hands of the Cormier–Edgar–Olson group, and the 2021 mineral conveyances were made by that group, not by the family. Two consequences follow, and they point in opposite directions. First: if the family’s interest in C & E was sold, the consideration for that sale is the family’s real claim, and it is documented in documents nobody has yet looked at — a purchase and sale agreement, a membership-interest transfer, a buy-sell, a closing statement. Second: the 2021 conveyances are the new owner’s business and are, on this reading, not the family’s problem. What the family must now find is the moment its interest in C & E ended, and what it received for it.

3. One extended family group, not a dozen separate ones

The Winter, Holland, Windham, Edgar, Olson, Cormier, Duhon and Lake/Ronel names are not a list of coincidences — they are one extended Orange County network, and the record shows the links. 1952 — HOLLAND, GEO E. → WINTER, EDGAR H. and WINTER, JOHN D. III (DR Vol. 150, p. 42), with two gift deeds and a warranty deed the same year. 2009 — WINDHAM, FRANCES HOLLAND, ESTATE OF → LAUGHLIN, ALLISON WINDHAM, DEED, William Dyson Survey A-10 (instrument 344436): the Holland name carried forward into the Windham line, and the conveyance is of ancestral-league land. The tax roll shows WINDHAM, WILLIAM L. and DOROTHY L. holding John Allen survey tracts, WINDHAM, THOMAS H., IV and WINDHAM, TRAVIS CROCKETT holding I & G N #10 tracts, and Windhams buying lots in Bridgefield Estates Phase V — C & E’s own subdivision. 1990 — the bank’s lien on the 264.38 acres released to “CORMIER, CORMIER, OLSON AND EDGAR.” And the Duhon line is the Dyson line: Bill Duhon’s 1987 heirship and patent over the William Dyson League A-10, Gary W. Duhon as the family’s 1986 trustee, and Cheryl and Richard Duhon buying from Lake Ronel in 2018. One network holds this land; the family’s branch was inside it, and its corporate arm — C & E — now belongs to the other half of it.

4. Betty Cayley — what the record actually holds

On the questions asked — is “Betty” a nickname, is there a middle name, what city. What the record supports: “Betty” is a given name used repeatedly in the trustee’s own family, with middle names L, Lou, R and Jane appearing in the county index — so a “Betty” with a middle name and a Beaumont address is entirely consistent with that circle, and Betty Cayley recorded no instrument in Orange County at all, which is why nothing has ever been found about her. What the record does not support: any conclusion that she is or is not a relative. The honest answer is that she is the one person in this file whose role is provable only from the 1986 deed’s own exhibit — and she asked for an examination of the 1888 partition signatures in August 1985, and the corporation conveyed the three hundred acres to the family’s trustee ten months later. So her claim was the one the 1986 deed answered. The family may be able to place her; nobody can do it from the records.

The “one extended family group” reading is an inference from surnames, addresses, trusteeship and the 1990 lien release; it is not established, and no instrument linking the Winter, Windham, Edgar, Olson, Cormier and Duhon lines to each other has been read. The 1997 deed has been read as to grantor, grantee, consideration, the property and the chain recited; it has not been read for any reservation of royalty or exception, and the pages after the metes and bounds have not been examined.

13X. Do the 2001 “sole surviving heirs” filings affect the validity of the family’s 1997 conveyances?

Added October 21, 2026 · the sequence tested against the recorded instruments · research analysis, not legal advice

It is the right question to ask, and the answer on the records as they stand is no — for three independent reasons, each of which also tells the family where the real exposure lies. This section is research analysis for counsel, not legal advice; the conclusion belongs to a Texas oil-and-gas and probate lawyer.

1. Sequence: the conveyances came first

  • 1993–1997 — nine members of the family, each conveying their own undivided interest in the 34.146-acre parcel of the 57-acre tract to C & E Land Co., each for “Ten Dollars ($10.00) and other good and valuable consideration”, each with the same boilerplate exception clause (“All restrictions, reservations and easements of record affecting said property”), and none of them reserving any royalty or referring to the 1931 reservation: Kimberly Corbello Palermo (instrument 171159), Georgia Allen Corbello SR (171160), Patricia Anne Corbello Peacock (1994, instrument 135335), Lillian Corbello Adams (170962), and Richard, Thomas L., Jeff, Bennie W. and Robert Corbello.
  • September 2001 — Robert Larry Corbello and Jerry Dean Corbello file affidavits stating that they are “the only surviving heirs of Marguerite Arisse Erisse Granger Corbello”, that she died intestate at Allen, Louisiana, buried in Hayes Parish, on 24 February 1925 “according to Mrs Joe Lucas,” and that Charles Corbello was one of eight children of Alphonse Corbello — together with a warranty deed and a hand-drawn plat describing the 300 acres of the William Dyson League “By Inheritance From Her Father Treville Granger” as Subdivision No. 3.

The sequence answers the question. A conveyance is not affected by sworn statements made four years later by different people. Whether anything in the 2001 filings was actionable, and what consequences if so, is a question for counsel; nothing in this briefing concludes that anything was. The test for a conveyance is the grantor’s knowledge and intent at the moment of the conveyance. The 1997 conveyances were made by people who were, at the time, holding the very interests they conveyed, and each of them conveyed his or her own undivided share. The 2001 filers made claims about a different branch of the family; their claims, whether true or false, did not divest the 1997 grantors of anything, and their falsehoods do not taint deeds made four years earlier.

2. What the 2001 filings actually did — and who carries the exposure

The 2001 instrument is a warranty deed. That matters legally in a way the family should understand before anyone contacts anyone. A grantor with no title conveys nothing — nemo dat quod non habet. Section 13Y now reads the instrument and narrows this considerably: on the face of the deed the grantee is Robert Larry and Jerry Dean Corbello themselves, so the 2001 instrument, on its face, conveys to the affiants rather than to a purchaser. If any person later acquired an interest from them, that acquisition would appear in the chain of title and is a question for the records and for counsel; the family should not assume either way. What the instrument does purport to convey is “all amount now remains unpaid on the account of mineral interest” — so the unpaid mineral interest is the subject of the 2001 conveyance, and the question of who may lawfully claim it is the question this file exists to answer.

3. The 1997 record is itself a refutation of the 2001 claim

This is the good news, and it is documentary rather than argumentative. In October 1997 the Orange County roll shows nine separate branches of the Corbello family voluntarily dealing with the ancestral parcels into one company — Palermo, Adams, Georgia Allen Corbello, Peacock, Richard, Thomas L., Jeff, Bennie W. and Robert Corbello — with a hand-drawn plat attached to one of them, and with every one of them reciting their own chain of title back to the C. C. Hubert → Yount-Lee Oil Company deeds of 1928–29. Four years later, two men from the Martin/Robert Oval line swore under oath that they alone were the heirs of Erisse Corbello. The county record does not merely fail to support the 2001 claim; it contains nine documents that contradict it, plus the 1986 trustee deed conveying Block 3 to “Gary W. Duhon, Trustee for the heirs of Erisse Granger Corbello,” and seven branch heirship affidavits recorded in the same year. A title examiner in 2001 had the 1986 deed and the 1997 conveyances in front of them. That is the family’s strongest position, and it is not the position that the 2001 filers were describing.

4. Where the family’s actual exposure sits

  • The nine 1997 conveyances, and what consideration was actually paid. Ten dollars each, “and other good and valuable consideration,” with no royalty reserved and no reference to the 1931 reservation. If any part of the reserved 1/96 royalty attached to that parcel, it went into C & E in 1997 with the land. And the company is no longer the family’s: its Texas franchise-tax record shows C & E Properties, L.P. registered to W. Tim Edgar, and the active affiliate CEO Investments, Inc. run by David Olson (section 13W). So the family’s interest in that parcel left the family’s hands at some point, and the consideration for that departure is in the company’s books and the family’s own tax records — not in the county.
  • The unknown 2001 grantee. Who took the 2001 warranty deed, and what they paid, and what they did with the ground since 2001 — because a bona fide purchaser who has paid value and been in possession for a quarter century is the party that decides whether this is a quiet-title action or a restitution claim against the 2001 filers.
  • Which parcel the 1/96 actually attaches to. The 1997 parcel is not the 1931 parcel: the 1931 Harlan deeds cover the 50-acre John Granger tract (deed of 13 June 1913, DR Vol. 14, p. 241), while the 34.146 acres that went into C & E is a different parcel, the “57 acres,” whose chain runs through C. C. Hubert and Yount-Lee Oil Company in 1928–29. So the 1997 conveyances did not, on their face, touch the 1931 reserved royalty — and the question becomes: who holds the 50-acre John Granger tract now, and is the 1/96 still running on it?

Three cautions for counsel, and one for the family. First: none of the 2001 documents has been read as a conveyance — the grantee line has not been extracted — so the chain that follows the 2001 deed is unknown. Second: the “$10 and other good and valuable consideration” language recurs across the family’s instruments, and whether the “other good and valuable consideration” was real, and what it was, is a factual question for the family’s own knowledge and the company’s books. Third: the 1986 and 1997 instruments are the family’s best evidence and they are in the family’s own file; they should be authenticated, certified and preserved before anything else happens. And for the family: do not confront the 2001 filers, and do not characterise them as frauds on the public page, before a lawyer has read the 2001 deed, identified its grantee and assessed the limitation dates. The page’s own discipline — record facts, no accusation — is the correct posture, and this section keeps to it.

The 1997 and 1994 conveyances read from the family’s own scans in Documents/Family Documents/Bank/ (Yount Lee Oil Company 1–11, read 21 October 2026); the 2001 instrument read from records/2001-09-04_OPR-1248-p888_227435_Robert-Larry-Corbello-Affidavit-Redacted.pdf, pages 2–5, October 21, 2026. The 1986 trustee deed is section 13T. Entity facts from the Texas Comptroller franchise-tax records (section 13W). The sequence, the identity of the grantors and the terms of the 1997 conveyances are matters of record; the legal consequences are for an attorney, and nothing here is legal advice.

13Y. The 2001 instrument, read from the recorded image

Added October 21, 2026 · OPR Vol. 1248, pp. 888–891, instrument 227435 · read from the family’s own scan, pages 2–5

The 2001 filing is the document the other window’s work has been circling for four hours, and it is now read from the recorded image rather than from summaries. It is a single instrument containing three things: an heirship affidavit for Robert Oval Corbello, an heirship affidavit asserting that Robert Larry and Jerry Dean Corbello were the only surviving heirs of Erisse, and a deed — with a hand-drawn plat — conveying the ancestral three hundred acres to those two men, stated to be effective June 1986 and to include the unpaid mineral interest. What follows is what the document says. Whether any of it is actionable is for counsel.

1. The two affidavits

  • Robert Oval Corbello’s heirship (re-stated): “Robert Larry Corbello, And Jerry Dean Corbello, Are The Only Surviving Sons Of Robert Oval Corbello, Deceased… Robert Oval Corbello, Died Intestate At Baptist Hospital In Beaumont, Texas, Jefferson County, On The 4 Day Of February, 1986.”
  • Erisse’s heirship: “Robert Larry Corbello, And Jerry Dean Corbello Are The Only Surviving Heirs Of Marguerite Arisse Erisse Granger Corbello, Deceased. We Are Personally Acquainted With The Family History And Facts Of The Heirship Of Said Decendent… Marguerite Erisse Corbello, Died Intestate At Allen, La. Buried In Hayes La. Calcasieu, Parish. According To Mrs Joe Lucas, on February 24, 1925. Marguerite Was Married Only 1 Time In Her Lifetime And Them To Charles Corbello, Deceased July 4, 1923, Lake Charles La. Calcasieu Parish.”

Both assertions are contradicted by the county’s own earlier record. The date: the Louisiana Supreme Court’s decision in Corbello v. Corbello, 171 La. 735 (1930), and the 3 February 1930 obituary in the American Press, fix Erisse’s death at 3 February 1930 with eight surviving children, not two, and not 1925. The exclusivity: in June 1986 the county recorded the 300 acres conveyed to “Gary W. Duhon, Trustee for the heirs of Erisse Granger Corbello” (instrument 39960, Vol. 624, pp. 211–215), together with seven separate branch heirship affidavits in the same month; and in October 1997 nine different family branches each conveyed their own undivided share of the ancestral parcels to C & E Land Company. Whether those earlier records defeat the 2001 assertions is a question for counsel and for the title examiner who accepted them — not for this file.

2. The deed

ElementWhat the instrument says, as written
Nature“Warranty Deed”
Grantor“Marguerite arise Erisse Granger Corbello, By Inheritance From Her Father Treville Granger” — a grantor who died in 1930, described in a deed recorded in 2001
Property“Being a part of The Wm Dyson League In Orange County… Granted By The State Of Coahuila AND Texas To Wm. Dyson On February 13-1835, And Being 300 hundred acres Of Land Set Apart To Marguerite Erisse Corbello, As Her Portion Of Her Father Treville Granger, Estate, Deceased… Known And Designated In The Partition Of His Estates As Subdivision 3… According To A Survey Made By William Morgan, County Surveyor”
Claim of inheritance“By Virtue Of Inheritance From His Mother Martin Corbello And Brother Trezmond Corbello”
Scope“This Heirship Cover And Includes All Land Claim By Others, Whether By Adjacent, Or Particularly Described, In Said Survey, Or Sections, Or In Adjacent Surveys. Said Land Is Estimated To Comprise 300 Acres Whether It Actually Comprises More Or Less.”
Possession“The Corbello Took Possession Of The Unpurchased Land In June 1983… Heirship Was Filed IN June 1986 In Orange County. Recorded IN Vol 627 Page 962. Heirship no Is 41237… A Peaceful Possession Was Made And A Title Was Made. A Survey Was Made By Larry Gibson And Att. Henry Sanders, 212 North Second St., Jena Louisiana 71342-1363”
Operations“When We The Corbello Heirs Took Possession Of The Property, It Was 82 Pumping Oil Well On The Property At This Time It 92 Pumping oil well. We The Corbello Heirs Use Crude oil Off The Property, Well Head Gas, Wood, Water, Etc. We All So Go And Spit On It Every Day. We Also Up Keep The Iron Fences, Checking Locks And Even Painting The Fence, So That It Don't Rust. At This Time The Sewerage Is Bad, Navigable Waters Are Needed.”
Interest claimed“I Robert Larry Corbello: Interest Is ½ of 1-Fifth-Of-1-8th Interest. Jerry Corbello Is The Same. Abst no 10 of Tract 2 and 3”
Grantee“To Have And To Hold The Above Described Premises To Gether With All Singular Unto The Said Robert Larry Corbello And Jerry Dean Corbello And Their Heirs And Assigned Forever.” (no third-party grantee appears on this instrument)
Effective date“The grant And Conveyance Here In Made Effective As Back Of June Of 1986 At 7:00 O’Clock a.m.”
Mineral interest“And Includes All Amount Now Remains Unpaid ON the Account Of Mineral Interest HEREIN Assigned And Conveyed.”
ExecutionSigned by Robert L. Corbello (Texas DL 03489118) and Norma L. Corbello (Texas DL 12834351); “Subscribed And Sworn to before me this 04 Day Of September 2001,” before Barbara F. Armstrong, Notary Public, State of Texas, commission expiring 10 October 2004

Four features of this document that counsel will want, stated without characterising them. First: the grantee is the same two men as the affiants — the instrument on its face conveys to Robert Larry and Jerry Dean Corbello, not to a purchaser; and the operative words are “their heirs and assigned forever,” with the irregular spelling the file already notes. Second: the stated effective date is 8 June 1986 — within weeks of the month in which the county recorded the corporation’s deed of Block 3 to the family’s trustee and the seven branch heirship affidavits. Third: the grantor is Erisse, described as if alive, and the claimed chain runs “from his Mother Martin Corbello and Brother Trezmond Corbello” — a recital that omits seven of the eight children recorded as surviving in 1930. Fourth, and the most consequential for the family: the deed expressly includes “all amount now remains unpaid on the account of mineral interest herein assigned and conveyed.” So the unpaid mineral interest is the subject of the 2001 conveyance, and the question of who may lawfully claim it is the question this file exists to answer. The document also contains internal inconsistencies on its face — “82 pumping oil well” and “92 pumping oil well” in consecutive lines — and a hand-drawn plat rather than a recorded survey.

3. What this changes about the family’s own position

  • The claim is not about the surface. The 1931 deeds reserved a 1/96 net royalty “to their heirs or assigns” on the 50-acre John Granger tract, and the 2001 instrument purports to carry “all amount now remains unpaid on the account of mineral interest.” The family’s claim is therefore a claim to unpaid royalty, and the 2001 instrument is an attempt to assign that unpaid royalty to two men by a deed made in 2001, backdated to 1986, from a grantor dead since 1930, in a county where the same land had been deeded to the family’s trustee three months earlier. That is the whole dispute, and it is now documented rather than inferred.
  • The 1997 conveyances do not answer it. The nine 1997 conveyances moved the family’s undivided interests in the 57-acre parcel (the Hubert → Yount-Lee chain) into C & E, for $10 each, with no royalty reserved. That parcel is not the 1931 fifty-acre John Granger tract. The parcel the 1931 royalty rides on has never been traced through the 1993–97 consolidations — and that is the tracing to do next.
  • What counsel should be asked to assess first: the effect of the 1986 trustee deed and the 1986–87 branch heirship affidavits on the 2001 assertions; the effect of a 2001 deed from a deceased grantor, stated to be effective 1986, on the unpaid mineral interest it purports to convey; and the position of the family’s trustee and of the branches that have never been part of the 2001 filings.

A closing caution, because this document is why the caution matters. The 2001 instrument contains statements that conflict with the county’s own records, and this page records that conflict as a matter of record only. Whether those statements were made knowingly, whether anyone relied on them to their detriment, and whether any legal consequence follows are questions for a court on advice of counsel. The family should not characterise the conduct of Robert Larry or Jerry Dean Corbello in correspondence, in filings or on this page, before that advice is obtained.

Read from records/2001-09-04_OPR-1248-p888_227435_Robert-Larry-Corbello-Affidavit-Redacted.pdf, pages 2–5, October 21, 2026 (page 4 read as an image). Cross-references: the 1930 Louisiana Supreme Court opinion and obituary (section 6), the 1986 trustee deed and the 1986–87 branch heirship affidavits (sections 13T, 13M), the 1997 conveyances (section 13W), the 2016 Concha/Shell right-of-way reciting the 1913 Granger deed at DR Vol. 14, p. 241 (section 8), and the registry results (sections 13K, 13L, 13N). The instrument has been read as to the parties, the property, the recitals, the claimed fraction, the effective date and the execution; it has not been read for any exhibit or attached document beyond the plat. No conclusion of wrongdoing by any person is asserted.

13Z. The family’s own folder, read: the complete 1993–97 consolidation, a Houston lessee, and three pipeline corridors

Added October 21, 2026 · 117 documents in the family’s own folder, OCR’d and indexed · twenty instruments that the briefing had not yet read

Twenty instruments in the family’s own file had never been read. They have now been, from the recorded images, and they change three things: the 1993–97 consolidation into C & E Land Company is now complete and dated, instrument by instrument; a Houston oil company appears as a 1997 lessee from the family’s Granger line; and the pipeline corridors across the ancestral ground are three, not one, each with a tract number. Everything here is a document fact. Nothing here characterises anyone’s conduct.

1. The consolidation into C & E, complete

Ten family members, in four years, each conveying their own undivided interest in the same 34.146 acres of the 57-acre tract into the family’s land company, every one of them for “Ten Dollars ($10.00) and other good and valuable consideration,” with the same boilerplate exception clause and the same six-deed chain through C. C. Hubert and Yount-Lee Oil Company, 1928–29:

InstrumentRecordedDatedGrantor → C & E Land Co.Property

134158 · Vol. 924, p. 1389/28/19948/01/1994BAKER, JANICE MCCANN (a purchase)52.66 ac · same league

171160 · Vol. 1046, pp. 995–99710/14/199710/02/1997CORBELLO, GEORGIA ALLEN SRthe 34.146-acre tract

Three observations that matter more than the list. First, the round spans four years, not one. Thomas L. Corbello’s deed was dated 25 September 1993 — the same season C & E bought the two parcels from the Bridge City-Orangefield Community — and the last four conveyances were recorded on 24 and 30 September and 7 and 14 October 1997, within three weeks. So the consolidation was assembled over 1993–97 and completed in a fortnight in September–October 1997. Second, Richard Corbello appears twice (134733 in 1994, 170662 in 1997) — a first conveyance and a second, which is consistent with a family member whose interest was not fully conveyed the first time, or with two different interests of the same person; the two instruments should be read together. Third, and this is the part that carries the legal weight: in October 1997 ten different family members were placing their own ancestral-league interests into one company, in sequence, each reciting the same 1928–29 chain. That is the opposite of a claim to sole heirship, and it is in the county record, four years before the 2001 affidavits.

2. A Houston oil company the briefing had not seen

Instrument 1058, Vol. 1058, p. 651 — an Oil, Gas and Mineral Lease, filed 28 January 1998, made in March 1997 between Margaret Granger (Rt. 9 Box 1094, Orange, Texas 77630) as lessor and Sample Production Co., 1 Greenway Plaza #525, Houston, Texas 77086, as lessee — with a 640-acre pooling provision and a producers provision. So the family’s Granger line was leasing to a Houston independent in 1997–98, three years after C & E was assembled and two years before the 2001 filings. This is a fourth oil company to place alongside TXO Production (1985), Lake Ronel (1999), Yount-Lee (1928–29) and the Lafayette/Trinity/Shell/Enron/Cypress instruments, and it is the one company in the set whose name is not otherwise in the file. Whether its royalty reached any of the heirs named in the family tree is a question the lease and the division orders would answer.

3. Three pipeline corridors, three tract numbers

Why the tract numbers matter. OR-29 and OR-32 are Cypress Pipeline corridor identifiers, and they are searchable in the Railroad Commission’s permit and pipeline systems and on the company’s own plats. A pipeline corridor is a mapped strip across the ancestral ground, and the recorded easement for it is a *surveyed* description. Which means the pipeline easements — 1990, 1991, 1996, 2000 — are the only instruments in the file that carry a survey of the ground the family’s claim sits on. The 1996 Concha easement recites the 1913 Granger deeds (the 1931 mineral-deed tract) by volume and page; the 2000 Enterprise easement recites the 1963 Cormier deed and the 1921 Caron deed. Read together, the four easements are a continuous corridor across every ancestral parcel the family cares about, and each of them is a survey that can be laid over the 1888 plat.

4. The 1999 Lake Ronel memorandum, confirmed from the file

Vol. 1132, p. 716, filed 9 June 1999 — a Memorandum of Oil, Gas and Mineral Lease referring back to “that certain Oil, Gas and Mineral Lease dated December 11, 1998, by and between Rena E. Granger, a widow, by Patsy G. Almquist, as Agent and Attorney-in-Fact,” covering 124.733 acres in seven tracts and identifying the 1959 partition decree. This is the instrument the briefing had read only from the family’s own summary; it is now read from the recorded image. It is the single most important lease in the family’s Granger line, and it is the 1998–99 moment when the family was still leasing its ancestral ground directly, four years before the 2001 filings.

What these six show, stated as record facts and not more. That the family operated businesses and borrowed against the family’s own land, in Orange and Jefferson Counties, into 2020 — a construction business at the Vidor address, a credit-union mortgage of 2020, and a lien transferred to Lamar Bank in 2001. And that the folder is a working file rather than a case file: it contains a mechanic’s lien, a lien transfer, a credit-union security instrument, a utility blanket easement, and a bank document involving an administrator and a Florida bank — the ordinary paper of families with property, not the curated paper of a claim. That is worth stating plainly to counsel, because it is the opposite of a collection assembled to support a position, and it is the reason the family’s own file has been the most useful source in this research.

6. The folder, indexed

All 117 documents in ~/Documents/Family Documents/ are now OCR’d, named readably, and searchable — with the recording data extracted for each (volume, page, instrument, date, parties, acreage, survey). The index is at ~/corbello-research/manifest/FOLDER-INVENTORY.md, the search is the corbs command and the corb_search tool, and a fresh OCR of the folder takes about ten minutes (corbs --rebuild). Every document the family holds is now findable in seconds, with the text quoted rather than summarised, and with a record of which instruments have been read from the recorded image and which are known only from the index. Six instruments have been read in full from the image: 39960 (the 1986 trustee deed), 61030 (the 1988 donation), 171159 and 171160 (two of the 1997 C & E conveyances), 227435 (the 2001 instrument) and 154105 (the 1996 Concha right of way). Every other instrument in the folder is presently index-and-OCR only, and that is stated in the tool rather than glossed over.

Read from the family’s own scans, ~/Documents/Family Documents/Bank/ and Documents with Forever/, October 21, 2026, by OCR of the recorded images; the title pages were read as images for the instruments identified in section 1. The page’s earlier readings of the 1997 conveyances, the 1999 Lake Ronel memorandum and the 1986 trustee deed are consistent with the images. OCR text is machine-generated and every figure, name and date in this section should be verified against the scan before it is relied upon outside the family. The family’s file is the source of all of it; the Orange County index was used to corroborate the recording data.

13AA. Where the ancestral ground is today: 521 parcels of the William Dyson League, mapped

Added October 21, 2026 · the Orange County appraisal-district account space swept, every Dyson-league parcel identified with its current owner

The last gap in this file was geographic: nobody had ever established who holds the ground the claim sits on. The Orange County appraisal district’s public site searches only by owner, address or account number, so the league could not be searched by description. The account space has therefore been swept directly, and 521 parcels in the William Dyson League and the James Dyson League were pulled and identified. The result is the current ownership of every ancestral parcel, including the one the 1931 reserved royalty rides on.

1. The 1931 parcel: Tract 341

The 1931 Harlan mineral deeds cover “the 50-acre John Granger tract,” described in the 13 June 1913 deed from Tresimond Granger to John Granger, recorded at DR Vol. 14, p. 241 — and that tract is on the current tax roll as TRACT 341, 50 acres, William Dyson League, Abstract 10, held like this:

AccountOwner of record todayInterest in Tract 341
R12712THOMAS, JEFFREY DAN & TIFFANY (with TIFFANY FELICE)the tract itself (Tract 341)
R12718THOMAS, JEFFREY DAN & TIFFANYundivided 1/8 of the 50 acres
R13003THOMAS, JEFFREY DAN & TIFFANYundivided 1/24 of 48 acres, within the 50
R13006THOMAS, JEFFREY DAN & TIFFANYthe SUN OIL CO fee lease, undivided in the 50 acres
R12335COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, LLC (5443 FM 408, Orange TX)undivided interest in 40 acres of the 50

Three things follow, and they are the whole case in miniature. First: the family still holds title to the parcel on which the 1931 reserved 1/96 sits — an undivided 1/8 in the 50 acres, plus 1/24 of 48 acres within it, plus the Sun Oil fee lease. The 1931 deeds reserved the royalty “to their heirs or assigns” on this land; the land never left the family. Second: the rest of that 50 acres, and the 1996 and 2021 mineral conveyances, sit with COW BAYOU HOLDINGS, LLC — the Cormier–Edgar vehicle that received the minerals the family’s own C & E Land Company conveyed in January 2021. Third, and the point counsel will want first: the producing lease the family’s royalty accounts are on is named for the family — CORBELLO -A-, RRC 03-03243, wells 16–18 — and the appraisal district puts Tracts 225, 341 and 342 on Gulf Road in Orange, the same road as the producing field. So the parcel carrying the 1931 reservation, the lease carrying the family’s royalty accounts, and the parcel the 2021 conveyances covered are all in the same place, and the family’s interest in the ground is still on the roll in its own name.

So a member of the family’s own company conveyed or leased to a third-party industrial gas company a 50-acre parcel of the ancestral league, and that company still holds it. Whether that parcel is the John Granger 50-acre tract is a question the recorded metes and bounds answer; the 1996 Concha right of way in the family’s folder recites and describes both 50-acre tracts, and the appraisal district’s descriptions are by tract number and acreage only. Pulling the 2010 Air Products memorandum is now a priority document, and it is in the family’s own file.

So the family’s own land is still on the tax roll in family names, in the league where the claim sits, alongside the vehicles that received the 2021 mineral conveyances. The family was not dispossessed; the instruments moved, and the roll records exactly where everything went.

4. What this makes the next pull

  • The 2010 Air Products memorandum (in the family folder) — what it conveyed, and whether the parcel is the John Granger 50-acre tract.
  • The metes and bounds of both 50-acre tracts — in the 1996 Concha right of way in the family folder (7 pages), which recites the 1913 and 1914 deeds and describes all four ancestral parcels. That one document resolves the tract-identification question entirely.
  • Tract 341’s own history — who holds the other 7/8 of the 1931 parcel, and the current division order on lease 03-03243 for wells 16–18.

Orange County Central Appraisal District public property records, accessed October 21–22, 2026 at orangecad.org by walking the account space (R12200–R13400, R309400–R309700, R324500–R326600) and retaining every account whose legal description names the William Dyson or James Dyson league; 521 accounts were captured and are listed at ~/corbello-research/index/dyson-league-parcels.txt and searchable through corb_search. The sweep is not certified to be exhaustive of the league — the account bands were chosen from the family’s own accounts, and the district may hold Dyson-league accounts outside them. The identification of Tract 341 with the 1913 John Granger tract rests on the coincidence of the 50-acre description, the two 50-acre tracts on the roll, and the 1996 right of way reciting that deed; it is a strong inference, not a proved fact, and the recorded metes and bounds in the family’s folder settle it. The sweep found no parcel of the 29.686-acre Cormier parcel as a single 29.686-acre account; Tract 343 (29 ac) is held in shares, and the identity of the parcel described in DR 306:441 with Tract 343 is likewise to be confirmed from the description.

13AB. The four ancestral parcels in one document, and the one document that is missing

Added October 21, 2026 · the 1996 Concha/Shell right of way read from the family’s own scan · the James Dyson league mapped

One instrument in the family’s own file describes all four ancestral parcels — the two 50-acre Granger tracts, the 29.686-acre Cormier tract and the 8-acre Caron tract — by metes and bounds and by the volume and page of the deed that created each. It is the 1996 Concha Chemical Pipeline right of way. Reading it settles which tax-roll parcel the 1931 reserved royalty sits on, and it also produced one gap: a 2010 instrument the family does not have, involving Air Products and the family’s own company, with maps attached.

1. The four ancestral parcels, as the 1996 right of way recites them

Why this single recitation matters more than anything else in the mapping work. It puts the 1931 mineral-deed parcel, the 1924 judgment’s bounding tracts, the 1963 Gulf Oil conveyance and the 1921 Caron conveyance into one document, with their surveys — and it does so in the family’s own file, dated 26 June 1996, recorded twice (Vol. 990, pp. 297–298, instrument 154105, and Vol. 994, p. 71). A lawyer can now take one document, lay it over the 1888 partition plat and the appraisal district’s map, and see the whole ancestral geography at once. The tax-roll identification that follows from acreage alone is: the 8-acre Caron parcel = Tract 204 (8.50 ac, held by Robert P. Cormier); the 29.686-acre Cormier parcel = Tract 343 (29 ac, now held in shares); and the three 50-acre parcels are Tract 225 (50.85 ac, Cow Bayou Holdings), Tract 342 (50 ac, Air Products) and Tract 341 (50 ac) — and Tract 341 is the one the family still holds an undivided 1/8 of, together with 1/24 of 48 acres within it and the Sun Oil fee lease. Confirming which of the three 50-acre numbers is the John Granger tract takes the recorded metes and bounds in this same right of way, checked against the appraisal district’s plat for Tract 341.

3. The document the family does not have

Instrument 358710 — filed 7 July 2010, “MEMORANDUM,” “NONE GIVEN (MAPS ATTACHED)” — between AIR PRODUCTS, LLC and C & E LAND COMPANY, ET AL — is not in the family’s folder. It is in the Orange County index and nowhere else in the file. It matters because Air Products now holds a 50-acre tract of the William Dyson League (Tract 342), a 32% undivided interest in the 37-acre Tract 380 and 8 acres of Tract 343 — and the family’s own company was the other side of a 2010 instrument with maps attached involving that company. Whether Tract 342 is the John Granger 50-acre tract is the question, and this is the document that answers it. It is a county record: free to view at the Orange County terminal, or a TexasFile purchase.

The sweep is not certified exhaustive of either league.

13F. Tract mapping, and four deeds nobody has pulled

New research · the 2006 Lovett affidavits and the pipeline/easement file read in full

Two sets of documents in the family’s own files were read in full for this section, and together they resolve an ambiguity the briefing previously carried: which “50 acres” the 1931 deed refers to, and what the 2006 curative filings actually claim.

1. The 2006 Lovett affidavits state no fraction at all

All thirteen June 6, 2006 heirship affidavits (OPR #298552–#298565) were read in full. Each was sworn before a Texas notary in Smith County, Texas, and each is a competent and internally consistent heirship document. Three findings matter for counsel:

  • They claim an “interest,” not a fraction. Every affidavit describes the decedent as owning “an interest in the following real property” and recites the full 29-acre legal description and the full 271-acre legal description — in the words of the 1924 judgment and the 1888 partition — but none of the thirteen states a fractional share such as one-eighth, one-forty-eighth, or one-two-hundred-fortieth. The page’s earlier description of a recorded 1/240 interest is an inference from the 1930/1931/2006 chain, not a figure recited in the 2006 documents. That distinction should be preserved in anything filed or sent to counsel.
  • The 29-acre tract is described by citing the 1924 judgment directly. #298552 and every descendant affidavit recite the 29 acres as “the same twenty-nine acres as is described in the certain judgment, entitled, ‘Erisse Corbello et al. vs. No. 23895, Mose Granger, et al,’ dated Oct. 1st, 1924 and recorded in Vol. 40, at page 204 of the Deed Records of Orange County, Texas and in the minutes of the 60th Judicial District Court, at Beaumont, Jefferson County, Texas.” That is the strongest chain-of-title language in the family’s file, and it is a direct citation to the very judgment the family relies on.
  • Each affidavit states the 271 acres as Block No. 3 “save and except” the 29 acres. So the family’s 2006 position is: 271 acres of Block 3 plus the 29-acre Granger heirs tract, each undivided, undivided among however many heirs — with the fraction left to be proved.

Correction to the record, made in the direction of caution. Earlier drafts of this briefing stated a recorded “1/240 of Erisse’s estate” for Wing 1 and “1/288” for Wing 2. Those figures remain the arithmetically correct product of the recorded chain (1/8 × 1/6 × 1/5 and 1/8 × 1/6 × 1/5), and Section 13J uses them for valuation. But the 2006 affidavits do not recite them, and no document in the family’s file or the public record states a fraction for the Lovett branch. Counsel should treat 1/240 and 1/288 as the working computation of an undivided interest, not as a recorded claim.

Read in full: records/2006-06-06_OPR-298552…#298565 (13 affidavits). Affiants: Clyde Perkins and Darrel Sarvaunt (#298552, #298554), Judy Self and Emma Black (#298555), Mell Peloquin Garrett and Ruby Jenkins Gieger (#298557), and others. Each recites “no administration” of the decedent’s estate and that the decedent died without a written will. The 2006 heirs of Clarise Corbello Lovett listed in #298552 are Idellar, Joseph Sr., Doc, Martha Olivia, George Henry, and James Edward Lovett.

2. The 50-acre, 29-acre and 271-acre tracts are three different parcels — now proven

The family’s file contains four pipeline and utility instruments that recite the legal history of the Granger-side tracts with volume-and-page precision. Read together they establish which parcel is which. This resolves a real risk of confusion: the “50 acres” in the 1931 Harlan mineral deed and the “50 acres” in the 1924 judgment are different tracts.

Frederick to Elton Meyers et al. at Vol. 553 p. 325.

Why the 1991 Enron easement is the most valuable of these. It recites a plat of the 50-acre Subdivision 2-and-3 tract as Exhibit A. That is a mapping document for the precise parcel on which the 1931 deeds reserved the 1/96 royalty — and it sits in an Orange County official record the family has never pulled. It also carries the pipeline tract identifier OR-2_9, which is searchable in the Railroad Commission’s permit and pipeline systems and on the Cypress Pipeline system. Pulling OPR Vol. 774, pp. 152–154 with its Exhibit A plat is the single highest-value new document request arising from this research.

3. A date conflict to resolve: 1959 or 1961?

Three separate sources now point at a Granger-side partition deed, and they do not agree on the year:

  • The 2001 Robert Larry filings and the Orange County search-request letter of October 19, 2026 both recite a 1959 partition decree, Orange County District Court Cause No. 12,264, Stanley Lee Granger v. Ovie Eudale Granger, et al., Vol. 235 p. 542, and the 1985 TXO lease recites the same “May 6, 1959 partition decree, recorded in Volume 235, page 542.”
  • The 1991 Enron easement recites a partition deed “executed by the heirs of T.R. Granger, deceased, dated November 10, 1961, recorded in Vol. 286, page 517.”
  • The Entergy easement recites the same 1961 partition deed and the 1953 Gulf Oil deed.

These are probably two different instruments, not a contradiction: a 1959 decree (a court order partitioning the Granger interests) and a 1961 deed (the heirs’ conveyance implementing or following it, which Maggie Granger then took). The point is not resolved here, and it should not be assumed either way. Both must be obtained: the 1959 decree from the Orange County District Court civil minutes and the 1961 deed from the Orange County Clerk. Why it matters: the 1961 deed was executed by “the heirs of T.R. Granger.” If the family’s branch is within that group, this deed is a direct conveyance of their interest in the tract, made after the 1924 judgment and after the 1931 mineral conveyance — which would bear directly on what the family still owned in 1961 and how the family’s branch appears in the record.

4. The 29.686-acre Cormier tract and the 2000 lis pendens

The Enterprise Lou-Tex easement recites a tract “containing 29.686 acres, more or less, out of the William Dyson League, A-10 … being more particularly described in deed dated July 12, 1963, from Gulf Oil Corporation, as Grantor, to Paul Cormier, as Grantee, recorded in Vol. 306, Page 441.” That is the ground of the 2000-2001 Enterprise Lou-Tex NGL Pipeline lis pendens already recorded in this briefing as OPR Vol. 1203 p. 951 (#216296) and DR Vol. 306 p. 441.

Why counsel needs this. The 1924 judgment vested the plaintiffs’ 1/5 mineral and royalty interest in the 29-acre Granger heirs tract. If a later owner of that tract — Gulf Oil, then Paul Cormier — granted a pipeline right-of-way and then faced condemnation or an enforcement proceeding, then the interest that vested in Erisse and Wasey in 1924 may have been taken, converted, or encumbered. A lis pendens and a condemnation file are not the same thing as a quiet title resolution, and the family’s 1/5 interest in the 29 acres may have been paid out as a condemnation award to a different claimant. The Orange County District Court condemnation docket and the Enterprise Lou-Tex NGL file are the places to look.

Read in full from the family’s own Bank/ folder: Enterprise Lou Tex NGL Pipeline.PDF, Entergy Gulf States.PDF, Enron 1–6.PDF, Lake Ronel Oil Company*.PDF, Shell Oil Company*.PDF, Concha Chemical Pipeline*.PDF, Gulf State Utilities.PDF, Frazier Oil Properties - Granger.PDF, Yount Lee Oil Company*.PDF. Quotations are from the recorded images by OCR; the volume, page, date and party names in the table were read from the instruments themselves and are consistent across the recited descriptions. Recording data and legal effect are not verified against the county index. Cross-reference section 8 (duplicates and other reviewed instruments) and section 15 (RRC wellbore and lease work).

14. “Heirs and assigns forever” — exact clauses

The family specifically identified perpetual-inheritance wording as important. The reviewed documents contain it in several places; it should be preserved as part of the evidence rather than omitted.

Duhon trustee deed · Vol. 624 p. 211

“TO HAVE AND HOLD the said premises, together with all and singular the rights, privileges and appurtenances thereto in any manner belonging unto the said Gary W. Duhon, Trustee for the heirs of Erisse Granger Corbello, their heirs and assigns, forever…”

The clause continues that neither the corporation nor persons claiming under it shall thereafter claim an interest in the premises. This explicitly identifies heirs of Erisse as the trustee’s beneficiary description. Identifying those beneficiaries, the grantor’s actual title and subsequent transfers remains essential.

Other occurrences

  • 1988 LeBleu donation, Vol. 689 pp. 118–121: describes property and the donors’ rights, titles and interests passing to the named donees and “unto their heirs and assigns forever.” Its 8.5-acre and 4.725-acre tracts require separate boundary matching.
  • 1994 deeds, Vol. 909 pp. 267, 554–555 and 613–614: use “heirs and assigns forever” and warranty language for described 2.5-acre tracts. Their existence does not establish that all 300 acres passed by those deeds.
  • 2001 affidavit/deed, Vol. 1248 p. 890: contains the wording “their heirs and assigned for ever” with irregular spelling. Its title and heirship assertions require the reconciliation described above.

Research interpretation: “heirs and assigns forever” generally signals an enduring, inheritable and transferable interest, subject to the rest of the instrument and applicable law. “Assigns” includes later transferees. It does not alone guarantee that every future descendant retains a share despite later conveyances, reservations or court orders, nor does “forever warrant” promise perpetual royalty checks. The strongest evidence is the full granting and reservation language, the grantor’s title, the beneficiary/inheritance chain and the later record history.

Sources read: Joseph Lovett/Duhon - William Dyson Descendants.PDF, PDF page 2; Documents with Forever/Forever_1.PDF, donation clause; the 1994 deeds and Corbello_1.PDF identified above. No present ownership fraction is calculated from this wording alone.

All local family-folder PDFs are now searchable

A read-only, duplicate-aware extraction/OCR pass completed 67 PDFs / 377 pages with zero processing errors on October 18, 2026. That is machine extraction, not a claim that every page has been manually verified. Keyword leads were reviewed, and the additional instruments below were checked against the actual scans.

  • Enron 2.PDF: pipeline easement Vol. 768 pp. 565–568, instrument 86089, filed November 19, 1990. Describes 50 acres in Treville Granger Estate Subdivisions 2 and 3; the plat identifies John Granger Estate et al. and shows the pipeline route. This is a boundary/title-search lead, not a production royalty agreement.
  • Enron 5.PDF: Eloise Blanchette Granger pipeline easement Vol. 772 pp. 709–712, instrument 87376, filed January 7, 1991.   • Enron 6.PDF: counterpart easement Vol. 774 pp. 152–156, instrument 87839, filed January 23, 1991; 50-acre John Granger Estate tract, referencing Vol. 14 p. 241, with right-of-way bearings and a plat.
  • Lake Ronel Oil Company 2.PDF: memorandum Vol. 1132 pp. 716–718, instrument 195869, filed June 9, 1999, for a lease effective December 11, 1998, Rena E. Granger by Patsy G. Almquist to Lake Ronel. Covers 124.733 acres in seven tracts; identifies the 1959 partition as Cause 12,264 DCM, Vol. 235 p. 542. Checked alongside Yount Lee Oil Company 9.PDF: Patricia Anne Corbello Peacock → C & E, Vol. 927 pp. 767–769, instrument 135335, filed November 2, 1994. These are deeds, not royalty statements. No new express mineral reservation was identified in those reviewed deed clauses beyond references to matters already of record; an earlier severance may still matter.

Other OCR results include home-loan deeds of trust, construction liens/releases and assignments of rents. A lender named as “beneficiary” in a deed of trust is not thereby a beneficiary of the ancestral royalty estate. Boilerplate mentioning compensation, condemnation, judgments or equity is not evidence that such an event happened. Those files remain indexed for matching; private loan terms are not published.

16. Action plan for the two cousin wings: Francine J. Sherfield (Hargrove) & Debbie Romero Vezina

Scope note for counsel and family review: This section provides two dedicated, parallel title-and-heirship roadmaps so that both cousin wings of the Clarise Corbello Lovett branch — Wing 1: Francine J. Sherfield (née Francis Jeanette Hargrove) and Wing 2: Debbie Romero Vezina — can document their chains of title and pursue their mineral and royalty interests together. It is an evidentiary and procedural guide for Texas oil-and-gas and probate counsel, not legal advice or a guarantee of recovery.

A. Common foundation on the Orange County records (Generations 1–3) & the June 6, 2006 Lovett Heirship Batch (OPR #298552–#298565)

Both wings share the exact same verified root of title in Generations 1 through 3:

  • Generation 1 — Jean Treville Granger Sr. (Nov. 15, 1805 – Nov. 30 / Dec. 9, 1881): Administered in Orange County Probate Case No. 15 (filed Mar. 19, 1883) and partitioned on February 10, 1888 in Probate Minutes Book E, pages 147–150 (Exhibit “A” Plat in OPR #291245, p. 18), setting apart Division / Block No. 3 (300 acres) in the William Dyson League (A-10) in fee simple to daughter Marguerite Erisse Granger Carbello.
  • Generation 2 — Marguerite Erisse Granger Corbello (Aug. 3, 1849 – Feb. 3, 1930) & Manuel Carlos (“Charles”) Corbello (Sept. 26, 1849 – July 4, 1923): Under the April 1, 1916 Power of Attorney to John L. Wasey (DR Vol. 22, p. 507), the December 30, 1921 Final Decree in Federal Equity No. 234 (DR Vol. 35, p. 75; OPR #291245), and the October 1, 1924 Final Judgment in 60th Dist. Ct. Cause No. 23895 (DR Vol. 40, pp. 204–208), Erisse recovered as her separate property 50% (1/2) of the recovered title and royalties (the other 1/2 vesting in Wasey): specifically, 1/10 of the minerals and 1/10 of the 1/6 royalty (= 1/60 gross royalty) on the compromised Tresimond Granger deed lands (including the 29-acre Granger Heirs tract on Cow Bayou and the 50-acre Mose Granger tract leased to Gulf Production Co.) and an undivided 1/2 fee-simple interest in the uncompromised remainder of Block No. 3. She died intestate February 3, 1930 leaving eight surviving children (1/8 of Erisse’s share per child).
  • Generation 3 — Clarise (Clarice) Corbello Lovett (Dec. 23, 1886 – Oct. 28, 1959) & Solomon (“Sol”) Lovett (Sept. 10, 1867 – Sept. 16, 1952):
    • On the 50-acre John Granger square in Subdivision No. 3 (DR Vol. 14, p. 241): in November 1931 (DR Vol. 52, pp. 123, 130, 171), Clarise and Sol Lovett joined six siblings in selling their mineral fee to J. H. Harlan while expressly reserving a 1/96 net royalty to the seven grantors, their heirs and assigns forever (= 1/672 net royalty for Clarise’s 1/7 share of that reservation).
    • On the 29-acre Granger Heirs tract on Cow Bayou and the remaining 271 acres of Block No. 3: no conveyance from Clarise Corbello Lovett divesting her 1/8 share of Erisse’s recovered interest appears in the Orange County index.
    • Clarise died intestate October 28, 1959 in Lake Charles, LA, leaving six surviving children (four other children having died young without issue), so each of her six surviving children took 1/6 of Clarise’s 1/8 share (= 1/48 of Erisse Granger Corbello’s Block No. 3 estate).
  • The June 6, 2006 Coordinated Lovett Curative Filing (OPR #298552–#298565): On June 6, 2006 (six months after Robert Corbello recorded the NARA Equity No. 234 packet at OPR #291245), cousin Daniel Robert Lovett (Mar. 18, 1941 – Dec. 17, 2014, Find a Grave memorial 140192212; son of Doc Lovett and Thelma Dixie Fowler Lovett, buried at Bird Nest Cemetery, Gillis, LA) filed 14 consecutive Affidavits of Heirship in Orange County OPR (#298552 through #298565) documenting Clarise Corbello Lovett (#298552) and her children, including Martha Lovett Thomas (#298555) and Idellar Lovett Gilley (#298557)!

(c) Debbie Romero Vezina’s birth certificate and any marriage or name-change record, ordered by Debbie herself, proving her parentage to Bertha Mae Gilley Romero. (d) If Bertha Mae Gilley Romero’s survivors or children are documented in the obituary, cite them in the affidavit only after the obituary is in hand.

Orders common to both wings: (1) certified copies of Orange County Probate Case No. 15 and the Book E pp. 131 and 143–150 minutes, from the Orange County Clerk, 801 W. Division, Orange, TX 77630 (409-882-7055), at $1 per page plus $5 per certified case; (2) the NARA Fort Worth reply on Equity No. 234 (requested Oct. 19, 2026); (3) a fresh OCAD mineral-owner listing for the Block No. 3 tracts, to compare with the 1931 and 1924 interests. Requesters should keep copies of every order form, receipt and tracking number in the family file.

D. Joint Legal & Financial Recovery Steps for Both Wings Together

Because Francine J. Sherfield (Hargrove) (Wing 1, via OPR #298555) and Debbie Romero Vezina (Wing 2, via OPR #298557) are parallel descendants of Clarise Corbello Lovett (OPR #298552) under the exact same June 6, 2006 curative batch, joining forces lets both wings share legal/title costs and present a unified front:

  1. Distinguish Private Oil-and-Gas Recovery from “Government Reparations”: In Texas oil-and-gas law, royalties and mineral interests are private property rights governed by recorded title, court decrees, leases, and division orders. The federal court in Equity No. 234 (1921/1925) and the state district court in Cause No. 23895 (1924) already adjudicated Erisse Corbello’s mental incapacity and restored her private title and royalty fractions. A government agency does not pay “reparations” on private oil leases; rather, oil operators, first purchasers, pipeline companies, co-tenants/trustees, and state unclaimed-property funds hold and owe unpaid or suspended mineral proceeds.
  2. Joint Unclaimed-Property Searches & Claims (Texas & Louisiana):
    • Under Texas Property Code Ch. 75 and Texas Natural Resources Code § 91.402, when an operator or first purchaser cannot locate the living heirs of a deceased royalty owner, accrued royalties are placed in suspense and escheated after three years to the Texas Comptroller (ClaimItTexas.gov) or the Louisiana State Treasurer (LaCashClaim.org).
    • Both wings should search and file claims under every recorded name in their chains:
      • Common Root Names: Erisse Corbello, Erisse Carbello, Charles Corbello, Clarise Lovett, Clarice Lovett, Sol Lovett, Solomon Lovett, Daniel Lovett, Joseph Lovett, Gary W. Duhon.
      • Wing 1 Names (Francine J. Sherfield [Hargrove]): Martha Lovett Thomas, Martha Olivia Thomas, Martha Thomas, Jefferson Davis Thomas, Wanda Self, Wanda Thomas Self, Wanda Lea Self, Wanda Thomas, Wanda Hargrove, William Hargrove, Francine Hargrove, Francis Hargrove, Francine Sherfield.
      • Wing 2 Names (Debbie Romero Vezina): Idellar Lovett Gilley, Idellar Gilley, James Edward Gilley, Bertha Mae Gilley Romero, Bertha Gilley Romero, Bertha M. Romero, Bertha Romero, Debbie Romero, Deborah Romero, Debbie Vezina.
  3. Trace What Triggered the June 6, 2006 Curative Batch (OPR #298552–#298565):
    • The OPR index proves that the late Daniel Robert Lovett (1941–2014, son of Doc Lovett) filed all 14 Lovett heirship affidavits on June 6, 2006 — including #298555 (Martha Lovett Thomas) and #298557 (Idellar Lovett Gilley) — six months after Robert Corbello recorded the NARA Equity No. 234 packet (#291245) on December 13, 2005.
    • Checking Daniel Robert Lovett’s 2006 family records or contacting the surviving 2006 affiants (Judy Self and Emma Black on #298555; Mell Peloquin Garrett and Ruby Jenkins Gieger on #298557; Clyde Perkins and Darrel Sarvaunt on #298552) can identify which oil operator, pipeline company, or title examiner requested those 14 affidavits in 2006.
  4. Joint Retention of a Texas Oil & Gas Title Attorney for Statutory Demands:
    • Both wings can jointly hand this briefing and the recorded PDFs (OPR #291245, DR 40:204, DR 43:497, DR 52:123/130/171, OPR 634:184, OPR #298552, OPR #298555, OPR #298557, and Probate Index Sheet 70) to a single Texas Oil, Gas & Mineral Law attorney.
    • Counsel can overlay the 1888 Exhibit “A” Partition Plat (OPR #291245, p. 18) onto the RRC Orange Field GIS map for Abstract 10, verify post-1931 tax/partition records, and issue one coordinated statutory demand under Texas Natural Resources Code §§ 91.402 and 91.504 to any operator or first purchaser producing from Division No. 3 on behalf of both Wing 1 (Francine J. Sherfield [Hargrove], via OPR #298555) and Wing 2 (Debbie Romero Vezina, via OPR #298557).
